- **Engine.** Logline record {protagonist, want, opposition, irony, genre, arena, comparables[]}. Buyer agents score fit against their slate gaps; the same fields seed coverage, one-sheet copy and later marketing hooks. If nothing reads irony, delete the field.
- **Takes.** 20 minutes to 3 days
- **Television.** A TV logline must also state the engine — what generates episode 2. Buyers reject 'movie-shaped' loglines; the engine field is required, not optional.

### Springboard pitch

A one-paragraph episode idea pitched aloud in the room. Twenty die for each one that lives. Killed ones go on the 'dead ideas' list and can be resurrected two seasons later.

- **Who.** Staff Writer through Co-EP pitches; Showrunner buys or kills
- **Decides.** Which of your generated springboards to pitch, in what order, whether to pitch it as your own or hand it to a struggling colleague.
- **Engine.** Per-writer springboard generator seeded by that writer's taste vector, credits and obsessions, filtered by bible constraints and a used-premise set. Showrunner accept probability = f(arc-slot fit, freshness, writer standing, room fatigue clock).
- **Takes.** 5–20 minutes each; a morning to generate a batch
- **Television.** Film has no direct equivalent; the film analogue is 'the take' pitched at a general or on an open assignment.

### The take

For an existing IP, book, remake or studio-owned title: the writer's specific angle, delivered verbally and often as a document, competing against other writers for the assignment.

- **Who.** Writer (father, or a hired writer the mother is considering)
- **Decides.** Which of the source's threads to make spine, what to cut, tone, period, POV character, and whether to bid at all against named rivals.
- **Engine.** Assignment competition object: N writers each holding a take vector; buyer preference vector; scoring with noise. Losing a bake-off costs weeks and reputation with that buyer, wins raise quote. Rival takes must be real objects, not flavour text.
- **Takes.** 1–3 weeks to prepare, 45-minute pitch
- **Television.** TV equivalent is the room pitch or the 'pitch on the book' with a bible and pilot story attached.

### Series bible / show document

Series document: premise, engine, franchise, character bios, world rules, tone, format and act structure, season one arc, episode springboards for eps 2–13, and the 'why now'.

- **Who.** Creator (father) writes; studio and network development execs (mother's side) annotate
- **Decides.** Engine type, franchise, number of leads, serialisation ratio, season length target, act architecture, and how many future episodes to expose.
- **Engine.** Bible object with typed sections; each section is read by something: engine type gates story-generation templates, franchise gates guest-cast archetypes, serialisation ratio drives per-episode arc/closed balance, character bios seed casting breakdowns.
- **Takes.** 2–6 weeks
- **Television.** Film's nearest equivalent is the treatment plus a mythology document on a franchise picture; a feature never needs an engine.

### Treatment

Prose retelling of the whole film in present tense, 3–30 pages, scene by scene without dialogue except the occasional line. A WGA-recognised writing step that can be paid separately.

- **Who.** Screenwriter (father); sometimes a hired treatment writer
- **Decides.** Scene order, which sequences exist, what is dramatised versus reported, where the act turns land, and the density of the telling.
- **Engine.** Ordered sequence list with per-sequence value charge and estimated page weight. The treatment must be convertible to a scene list — the outline step reads it — and its estimated page weight must predict the eventual draft length within tolerance.
- **Takes.** 2–4 weeks (est.)
- **Television.** TV uses a 'story area' (1–3 pages) before the outline instead of a long treatment.

### Beat sheet

A numbered list of story beats with target page numbers, mapped to whichever structural model is in use. The instrument that turns structure from a feeling into a checklist.

- **Who.** Screenwriter; in TV, the room collectively
- **Decides.** Which structural model to work in, and the page number each named beat is aimed at.
- **Engine.** Beat template library (Save the Cat 15, Field paradigm, Sequence 8, Harmon 8, Truby 22, Story Grid, Freytag 5, kishōtenketsu 4). Each template = ordered beats with target page as a fraction of runtime. Draft pagination is diffed against targets to produce a structure report.
- **Takes.** 3 days to 2 weeks
- **Television.** TV beat sheets are per-act and per-story-line: A story beats, B story beats, runner beats, each with an act assignment.

### The board

Index cards, one scene per card, pinned in rows — traditionally four rows of ten to fourteen for a feature. Cards get moved, colour-coded by storyline or POV, and pulled.

- **Who.** Screenwriter alone (film); the room together (TV)
- **Decides.** Scene order, which storyline owns each card, what gets pulled entirely, whether to run parallel rows per character.
- **Engine.** Card objects {scene stub, storyline id, POV, est. eighths, value charge in/out}. Reordering must recompute act boundaries, page targets and information-reveal validity — a card moved before the reveal it depends on must flag a continuity break.
- **Takes.** 2 days to 2 weeks
- **Television.** TV boards are whiteboard columns, one per act, with the act-out written at the bottom of each column in a different marker.

### Breaking story

The room talks an episode into existence beat by beat, act by act, until the board is full and the act-outs land. Days of argument, tangents, food, and 'what if she just doesn't answer'.

- **Who.** Whole room: Showrunner leads, Co-EPs steer, Story Editors and Staff Writers pitch, Writers' Assistant records
- **Decides.** Every beat, the act-out for each act, which story the teaser belongs to, whose runner survives, and when the room has 'broken it' versus needs another day.
- **Engine.** Room simulation: per-writer pitch rate and quality drawn from their attributes, showrunner filter, room fatigue and morale clocks, a beat board that must reach completeness (all acts filled, act-outs charged, A/B/C braided) before the episode can go to outline.
- **Takes.** 2–5 days per episode; longer for premieres and finales
- **Television.** This process is television. Features break story alone or in a two-hander with a producer; nothing in film has the room's throughput or politics.

### Act-out design

Build the last beat of each act so the audience cannot leave: reversal, reveal, threat, or question. Written to land on the page as the last line before the act break.

- **Who.** Showrunner and the assigned writer
- **Decides.** Which beat is strong enough to be an act-out, whether to spend a big reveal early, and whether the act-out is plot, character or dread.
- **Engine.** Act-out strength score from beat type, reversal magnitude, and whether it resolves or opens a question. Feeds retention: a weak act-out raises tune-out probability at that break in the audience model. If nothing reads the score, the act break is decoration.
- **Takes.** Minutes to a full day per break
- **Television.** Film has no act-outs; its analogue is the sequence-end tension curve, which affects pacing notes, not tune-out.

### Cold open / teaser decision

Choose whether the episode opens before main titles, and whether that opening is a teaser (in-story hook) or a cold open (self-contained comic or tonal piece). Length typically 1–4 pages.

- **Who.** Showrunner, with the network
- **Decides.** Teaser, cold open, or straight into Act One. Whether the teaser is the A story, a victim-of-the-week, or an unrelated stinger.
- **Engine.** Episode template holds an optional pre-title block with type, page budget and story ownership. Runtime accounting must subtract it from act budgets; audience model must treat the first 90 seconds as the tune-in decision window.
- **Takes.** Decided at break; written in hours
- **Television.** Wholly televisual. Features open on page one; the 'teaser' vocabulary only appears in film for pre-title sequences (Bond, Indiana Jones).

### Tag decision

A short scene after the last act: comic button, emotional coda, or next-episode setup. Half a page to two pages, after the final commercial break in broadcast.

- **Who.** Showrunner
- **Decides.** Whether to have one, and whether it resolves the episode, teases the next, or is a joke that pays a runner.
- **Engine.** Tag block with type {button, coda, tease}. A tease type must write a promise object that the next episode's break is checked against; a runner-paying button must reference the runner it closes or the reference dangles.
- **Takes.** Minutes to a day
- **Television.** Film's equivalent is the post-credits stinger, which is a marketing artefact rather than a structural slot.

### Story braid

Assign each storyline a letter — A the largest, B, C, sometimes D — plus runners (small recurring jokes or beats). Braid them so scenes alternate and each act carries at least two.

- **Who.** Showrunner and room
- **Decides.** Which story is A this week, which regular gets the B, whether the C is plot or character, how many runners, and which characters are 'out' this episode.
- **Engine.** Storyline objects with page share, character assignments and act coverage. Constraints: cast utilisation (guaranteed episode counts per series regular contract), page share bands per line, and no act with only one line. Violations produce real notes.
- **Takes.** Decided during the break
- **Television.** Film uses A/B plot but never letters-per-episode with contractual cast utilisation attached.

### Outline

Scene-by-scene prose, 6–15 pages for an hour of TV, longer for film, with act headers, slug-style scene identifiers and the beats spelled out. The document notes are given on before a page of script exists.

- **Who.** Assigned writer drafts; Showrunner rewrites; studio and network approve (TV). Film: writer delivers to producer/studio
- **Decides.** Scene granularity, how much dialogue to seed, whether to show your act-outs in full or hold them, and which beats to leave vague to avoid a note.
- **Engine.** Outline = ordered scene stubs each carrying heading fields, characters, beat ids, est. eighths. Approval gate holds a note set; unaddressed notes at the next step raise rejection probability. Outline scene count must predict draft scene count.
- **Takes.** TV: 3–10 days to write, plus approval rounds. Film: 2–6 weeks
- **Television.** TV outlines are contractually gated: the company has 14 days from delivery to elect to acquire the outline and employ the writer for teleplay. Film outlines are usually inside the first-draft step.

### Script assignment

Deciding which writer gets which episode, alone or as a paper team of two. Story Editors and above carry WGA credit guarantees; Staff Writers are not guaranteed a script in the same way.

- **Who.** Showrunner assigns; WGA and the studio track
- **Decides.** Whether to take the episode yourself, hand a premiere or finale to a favourite, pair two writers, or give a Staff Writer their first.
- **Engine.** Assignment ledger per season with per-title guarantees, script fee obligations (separate from weekly salary above Story Editor in some deals), and a fairness/politics model where repeated snubs damage room morale and raise staff-departure probability.
- **Takes.** An afternoon, usually at the start of the season
- **Television.** Purely TV. Film's analogue is the studio hiring a rewrite writer, which is a different contract and a different credit risk.

### Structural model selection

Pick the underlying architecture the script is built on and map beats to it. Different models put turns at different pages and produce measurably different shapes.

- **Who.** Writer (father) chooses; showrunner or studio may impose one
- **Decides.** Which model, and whether to declare it (some execs ask) or work it silently.
- **Engine.** Each model is a beat template with ordered named beats and target positions as fractions of total pages. Choosing a model changes where reversals land, which changes pacing scores, note generation and audience engagement curves. Two models must not produce identical page maps.
- **Takes.** An hour to decide, weeks to execute
- **Television.** TV layers a series architecture (procedural, serialised, hybrid, anthology) on top of the episode model; the series architecture governs how much of each episode must close.

### Element typing

Every line in a screenplay is one of a finite set of typed elements. The software auto-advances between them; the type determines margins, capitalisation, and how the line paginates.

- **Who.** Writer at the keyboard; Script Coordinator enforces in production
- **Decides.** What each line is: scene heading, action, character, dialogue, parenthetical, transition, shot, general/text, dual dialogue, or act/scene marker.
- **Engine.** Document is an ordered list of typed Elements with stable ids, not a text blob. Every downstream system — pagination, breakdown, sides, revision marks, dialogue counts, credit arbitration word counts — reads element type. A blob makes all of them impossible.
- **Takes.** Continuous
- **Television.** Multi-camera adds mandatory act and scene marker elements and its own capitalisation and spacing rules for action and stage directions.

### Slug line construction

INT./EXT. + LOCATION + TIME OF DAY, all caps, one line. The most load-bearing line in the document: it creates a scene, a set, a company move, a lighting condition and a line item.

- **Who.** Writer; Script Coordinator standardises before lock
- **Decides.** Interior or exterior, the exact location string, the time of day token, and whether this is a new scene or a continuation of the last.
- **Engine.** Heading parses to fields: {int|ext|int-ext|est, location id resolved against a set/location registry, time token, continuity flag}. Location string must resolve to a real set or location object; a new string creates a new set with cost and build implications. Time token drives daylight, crew hours and lighting package.
- **Takes.** Seconds each; hours of standardisation before lock
- **Television.** TV reuses standing sets constantly; a slug naming a set that is not standing is an immediate cost note from the line producer that a feature would never generate.

### Sub-headings and secondary slugs

Short capitalised location fragments inside a scene (KITCHEN, THE DOOR, BACK TO SCENE) that move the camera without creating a new numbered scene.

- **Who.** Writer
- **Decides.** Whether a move is a sub-heading or a new scene — which decides whether it gets a scene number, a breakdown row and a schedule line.
- **Engine.** Sub-headings attach to their parent scene and do not mint scene numbers, but must still register set-area usage for the breakdown. Getting this boundary wrong changes the scene count, which changes the schedule, which changes the budget. Model the boundary explicitly.
- **Takes.** Seconds
- **Television.** None significant; multi-cam instead uses lettered scenes for each new set.

### Action writing

Present-tense description of what is seen and heard. Paragraphs kept short — three to four lines — because white space controls reading speed and therefore perceived pace.

- **Who.** Writer
- **Decides.** Density, paragraph length, whether to write camera or imply it, how much interiority to smuggle in, sound cues in caps.
- **Engine.** Per-scene action metrics: words, lines, mean paragraph length, sensory-cue counts, and a density ratio against dialogue. Density must feed the page-to-runtime estimate (dense action pages run long on screen; sparse pages run short) and the readability score used in coverage.
- **Takes.** Continuous
- **Television.** Multi-cam writes action in ALL CAPS and much shorter; single-cam TV action is written leaner than features because schedules are tighter.

### Character introduction

The first appearance of a speaking character is capitalised in action, with an age and a compressed description. That line becomes the casting breakdown almost verbatim.

- **Who.** Writer
- **Decides.** Age, physical description or its absence, ethnicity specification or its absence, and the one behavioural detail that defines them.
- **Engine.** Introduction line generates a Character record {name, stated age band, described attributes, first-appearance page}. Casting reads it: a specified age band narrows the audition pool and changes availability, quote and package. If casting ignores the line, the line is decoration.
- **Takes.** Minutes per character
- **Television.** In TV the pilot's introductions create series regulars, which create series-regular contracts, episode guarantees and options — a far heavier consequence than a feature role.

### Dialogue writing

Character cue in caps, dialogue in a narrow column. Roughly 35 characters per line at standard margins, which is why long speeches eat pages and why the column width is itself a discipline.

- **Who.** Writer; in the room, sometimes pitched aloud line by line
- **Decides.** Who speaks, in what order, length of speeches, overlaps, interruptions with dashes, silence.
- **Engine.** Per-character line counts, word counts and speech-length distribution per scene and per episode — read by cast utilisation, by actors' agents evaluating a role's size, by the arbitration word-share ledger, and by table-read timing. Also drives ADR and looping load later.
- **Takes.** Continuous
- **Television.** TV tracks per-episode line counts against series-regular expectations and contracts; a regular with three lines generates an agent call that features do not have.

### Character cue extensions

Parenthetical tags after the character name: (V.O.), (O.S.), (O.C.) in multi-cam and TV, (CONT'D), (FILTERED), (PRELAP), (SUBTITLED). They tell post and sound what the line is.

- **Who.** Writer; Script Coordinator polices consistency
- **Decides.** Whether a line is in the room, off screen, in the head, through a phone, or laid over the previous scene.
- **Engine.** Extension is a field on the dialogue element, not decoration in the name string. V.O. and FILTERED route to different recording plans (booth vs. on-set), affect whether the actor is needed on the day, and change the sound department's deliverables list.
- **Takes.** Seconds
- **Television.** (O.C.) 'off camera' is the multi-cam and TV term where features write (O.S.).

### Parentheticals

Wrylies — short direction inside the dialogue block. Contested territory: writers use them for clarity, actors read them as being told how to act.

- **Who.** Writer; frequently deleted by the director or actor
- **Decides.** Whether to specify delivery, to whom a line is spoken, or a small physical action mid-speech.
- **Engine.** Parenthetical objects with type {delivery, address, action, pause}. Delivery parentheticals should raise friction with high-status actors in the performance model and lower ambiguity in the read; address parentheticals resolve who is being spoken to, which the eyeline and coverage plan needs.
- **Takes.** Seconds
- **Television.** Multi-cam uses them far more heavily and treats them as house style rather than intrusion.

### Dual dialogue

Two dialogue blocks side by side to indicate simultaneous speech. Paginates as one block and is a formatting special case that breaks naive page engines.

- **Who.** Writer
- **Decides.** Whether an overlap is written as dual dialogue, as an interruption, or as action describing the overlap.
- **Engine.** Dual-dialogue node holds two child dialogue blocks measured at half column width; page-break rules must handle it atomically. Sides generation and line counts must attribute both halves correctly or an actor's part size is wrong.
- **Takes.** Seconds
- **Television.** Rare in multi-cam, common in ensemble single-cam comedy.

### Transitions

CUT TO:, DISSOLVE TO:, SMASH CUT TO:, MATCH CUT TO:, FADE IN:, FADE OUT., FADE TO BLACK., INTERCUT WITH:, END OF ACT ONE. Right-aligned except FADE IN.

- **Who.** Writer; largely stripped in spec drafts, restored in shooting scripts
- **Decides.** Whether to mark a transition at all, and which — a DISSOLVE claims time has passed, a SMASH CUT claims violence of contrast.
- **Engine.** Transition element with type; editorial reads it as an intent (not a mandate) and the post model uses it for optical/VFX counts — a DISSOLVE is an effect with a cost and a duration, FADE TO BLACK at an act break is the broadcast handoff.
- **Takes.** Seconds
- **Television.** Broadcast TV requires explicit END OF ACT / ACT TWO markers; features never carry them. Streaming scripts often keep act markers internally and strip them for the reading copy.

### Shot calls

ANGLE ON, CLOSE ON, INSERT, POV, PUSH IN, PULL BACK, AERIAL, HANDHELD, SERIES OF SHOTS, TIGHT ON, WIDE. Directing on the page; a spec red flag, a shooting-script necessity.

- **Who.** Writer (sparingly in spec), Director in the shooting script
- **Decides.** Whether the writer specifies coverage, and which shots are load-bearing enough to write (an INSERT of a note the plot needs, a POV that is the story).
- **Engine.** Shot elements register as required setups in the coverage plan: an INSERT is a separate setup with its own time cost; a POV constrains lens and camera position. A writer who over-specifies raises director friction; a writer who specifies nothing loses control of a reveal.
- **Takes.** Seconds each
- **Television.** TV shooting scripts carry fewer written shots because episodic directors are hired to serve the show's established visual grammar.

### Montage and intercut

MONTAGE / SERIES OF SHOTS with lettered or dashed sub-items, and INTERCUT for cross-cut conversations. Both compress large amounts of screen time into few pages, or few pages into much screen time.

- **Who.** Writer
- **Decides.** Montage versus discrete scenes, how many beats in the montage, and whether an intercut is written once or as alternating slugs.
- **Engine.** Montage node with child beats, each carrying its own location and set requirement — the breakdown must explode them into individual shooting elements even though they occupy one page. Runtime estimate for montage must be decoupled from page count (music length governs).
- **Takes.** Minutes
- **Television.** TV montages are budget events: five locations in half a page is a company-move nightmare the line producer will note.

### Pagination

Laying elements onto US Letter pages in 12-point Courier: 1.5" left margin, 1" right, 1" top and bottom, dialogue at 2.5" from the left edge, parentheticals at ~3.1", character cues at ~3.7". Roughly 55 lines per page.

- **Who.** The software; Script Coordinator arbitrates in production
- **Decides.** Nothing directly, but the writer manipulates it constantly — cutting a word to save a line, to save a page.
- **Engine.** Real line-metric model: Courier is 10 chars/inch, 6 lines/inch, so each element type has a fixed wrap width in characters. Page breaks obey widow/orphan rules, (MORE)/(CONT'D) on split dialogue, no break after a scene heading. Page count must be computed, never authored.
- **Takes.** Instant, continuously
- **Television.** Multi-cam double-spaces dialogue and capitalises action, roughly doubling page count for the same runtime: a 22-minute multi-cam script runs ~45–58 pages against ~28–34 for single-cam.

### Page-count to runtime estimation

The one-page-one-minute convention, and every place it fails. Used to decide whether a script is long, and whether to cut before shooting or in the cut.

- **Who.** Writer, Showrunner, Line Producer, and later the Editor
- **Decides.** Whether to cut pages now, shoot long and fix in post, or let it run.
- **Engine.** Estimator per scene: base minutes from page-eighths, adjusted by dialogue/action density ratio, montage flags, action-sequence flags, and per-project style factors learned from that director's previous cuts. Must produce a distribution, not a point value, because the whole cutting decision hangs on the spread.
- **Takes.** Minutes to compute; argued about for weeks
- **Television.** TV must hit a slot: broadcast hour ~42–44 min of content, half-hour ~21–22 min; premium/streaming is elastic. Overlength in broadcast is not an artistic problem, it is a delivery failure.

### Page-length targeting

Aiming the draft at a length: features 95–120 pages, comedy shorter, spec scripts penalised above ~120, studio tentpoles routinely longer, TV per format.

- **Who.** Writer, with studio expectations
- **Decides.** Target length, and whether to fight the studio's number.
- **Engine.** Target band per format and genre with a penalty curve: coverage scores, buyer read-through probability and budget estimates all move with page count. An over-long script must raise the budget estimate and the pass probability, or the number is decoration.
- **Takes.** Set at outline; enforced through drafts
- **Television.** TV lengths are hard: hour drama 52–62 pages, half-hour single-cam 28–34, multi-cam 45–58. A 70-page hour script is not ambitious, it is a rewrite order.

### First draft

Write the script from the approved outline. In TV, the writer's draft goes to the showrunner first and is often heavily rewritten before anyone else sees it.

- **Who.** Screenwriter (father) alone; or the assigned TV writer under a deadline
- **Decides.** Whether to follow the outline exactly, where to deviate, how much time to spend, and whether to over-deliver length.
- **Engine.** Draft generation from beats: quality is a function of writer attributes, time spent, outline quality, fatigue, and interference. The result must be a real document object with scene list and page count, not a quality score, because everything downstream reads its contents.
- **Takes.** Film: 6–12 weeks typical; contractually, non-delivery within 9 weeks of commencement triggers a partial payment. TV episode: 1–3 weeks
- **Television.** The TV writer's draft is a step, not the script. It becomes the showrunner's draft, then the studio draft, then the network draft, then production.

### Reading period and steps

After delivery, the company reads for a contractual period before electing to proceed to the next paid step, cut the writer loose, or set a rewrite. Steps are the writer's income.

- **Who.** Studio/network executives (mother's side) and Business Affairs
- **Decides.** As the buyer: proceed, pass, or change writers. As the writer: whether to do unpaid work to protect the next step.
- **Engine.** Contract object with ordered steps, each with fee, commencement and delivery dates, reading period, and an option to proceed. Timers must actually run in the calendar and payments post to the ledger. Free rewrites are prohibited under the WGA MBA — model the pressure and the violation.
- **Takes.** 14 days for a TV outline election; theatrical reading periods vary by deal (est. 2–6 weeks)
- **Television.** Episodic TV runs on 'story with option for teleplay' structures and much shorter clocks; features run on multi-step deals with long gaps.

### Notes intake

Receiving written notes on a draft, sorting them by who can enforce them, and deciding which to take, which to fake, and which to fight.

- **Who.** Notes come from: studio exec, network exec, showrunner, director, star, financier, producer, S&P
- **Decides.** Per note: take it, take a version of it, ignore it, or escalate. Also who to call first.
- **Engine.** Note object {source, authority level, specificity, correctness, cost to implement in pages and days}. Unaddressed notes from high-authority sources raise the probability of rewrite orders, writer replacement, or the project stalling. Some notes must be genuinely wrong and cost quality if taken.
- **Takes.** Notes arrive 1–4 weeks after delivery; the pass takes days to weeks
- **Television.** TV notes come at outline, writer's draft, studio draft, network draft, table read, and again after the producer's cut — six gates against a feature's two or three.

### Notes call

The verbal version of the notes, where the real note is often different from the written one, and where the writer's job is to leave with a clear list and their spine intact.

- **Who.** Writer, producer, and two to six executives on speakerphone
- **Decides.** Which notes to argue on the call versus concede and quietly not do, and whether to volunteer a solution or take it away.
- **Engine.** Dialogue-tree negotiation with per-executive positions, hidden true concerns, and an authority ranking. Outcome sets the binding note list. Arguing well raises standing with some execs and lowers it with others — the model must let a writer win the call and lose the job.
- **Takes.** 45–90 minutes
- **Television.** On a series the showrunner takes the network call and shields the room; a staff writer may never speak to the network at all.

### Rewrite pass

A specific kind of revision: page-one rewrite, structural pass, character pass, dialogue pass, polish, trim pass, or production rewrite. Each touches different elements and carries different credit consequences.

- **Who.** Original writer, or a new writer hired over them
- **Decides.** Which pass, how deep, whether to keep the previous writer's scenes, and whether to rewrite enough to claim credit.
- **Engine.** Each pass type has a distinct element-mutation profile: a polish changes dialogue lines only, a page-one replaces scene objects wholesale. The word/line delta per pass feeds the arbitration ledger, so the credit outcome falls out of what was actually changed.
- **Takes.** Polish: 1–2 weeks. Rewrite: 4–8 weeks. Page-one: 8–12 weeks
- **Television.** In TV the showrunner rewrites every script as a matter of course and does not usually take credit for it; in film the same work would trigger arbitration.

### Table read

The full script read aloud around a table, timed, with a reader for stage directions. In TV it is a weekly institution and a public verdict on the draft.

- **Who.** Cast, writers, director, executives, department heads; Script Coordinator runs the book
- **Decides.** Casting last looks, which jokes survive, whether a scene is cut on the spot, and how much trimming is needed.
- **Engine.** Timed read: per-line duration from word count and character delivery speed, plus laughter gaps in comedy. Produces a measured runtime, a per-joke response record, and executive reaction. Both the runtime and the response must drive the post-read rewrite.
- **Takes.** 1–2 hours plus notes session after
- **Television.** Multi-cam reads Monday, rewrites Monday night, runs through Tuesday–Wednesday, shoots Friday. Features table-read once, if at all, weeks before production.

### Post-table-read trim

Cutting to time and to notes immediately after the read. In broadcast a script four minutes heavy must lose roughly four pages before the shooting draft goes out.

- **Who.** Showrunner and the room, overnight
- **Decides.** Which scenes die, which speeches shorten, whether to cut a storyline entirely to protect the A story.
- **Engine.** Trim solver over scenes and speeches with per-item runtime savings and per-item story damage. Cutting a scene must break anything that referenced it — a cut setup leaves an unpaid payoff, and the engine should surface that as a continuity fault rather than silently allowing it.
- **Takes.** An evening to two days
- **Television.** Feature trims happen in the edit instead, at far higher cost, because the pages were already shot.

### Punch-up

Line-by-line joke replacement. The room reads a scene, pitches alternates for every weak line, and the showrunner picks. Alt jokes are also written to be shot as protection.

- **Who.** Whole comedy room, often at night; sometimes hired punch-up writers
- **Decides.** Which lines to open up, how many alts to shoot, whose joke goes in.
- **Engine.** Per-line joke score with alternates; each alt is a real dialogue variant attached to the line, shootable as a separate take with time cost. Editorial later picks among the shot alts — so the punch-up genuinely changes the finished episode, not just the page.
- **Takes.** An evening; on multi-cam, most of the week
- **Television.** Features do punch-up in a compressed hired-gun week; comedy series do it every episode as standard practice.

### Run-through rewrites

Rehearsal on the standing sets in front of writers, twice or three times before the audience shoot, each followed by an overnight rewrite and new coloured pages the next morning.

- **Who.** Multi-cam room, director, and cast on the stage
- **Decides.** What survives contact with the actors, staging changes that require rewrites, and which scenes get restaged.
- **Engine.** Rehearsal event producing per-scene performance readings and staging problems; the overnight rewrite generates a new revision set with its own colour, distributed by call time. The weekly colour cadence should be a visible, playable rhythm.
- **Takes.** Four to five days per episode, cyclically
- **Television.** Exists only in multi-camera television and in theatre. Single-cam and film have no equivalent.

### Studio and network draft approvals

The draft passes through two separate note gates with different priorities — the studio protects cost and franchise, the network protects the slot and the brand.

- **Who.** Studio execs, then network/platform execs; showrunner delivers
- **Decides.** How much to change between the two gates, and whether to play one against the other.
- **Engine.** Two note sources with different weight vectors (cost, cast, tone, brand, standards) and different escalation power. Approval flags gate the production draft: an unapproved script cannot lock, which delays the schedule and costs money.
- **Takes.** 3–10 business days each (est.)
- **Television.** Film has one buyer chain; TV has at least two, and on a co-production three or four, each with veto.

### Standards and Practices pass

Line-by-line review for language, violence, sexual content, drug use, brands, imitable behaviour and defamation, returning specific mandated changes with alternates permitted.

- **Who.** Broadcast Standards & Practices executive; writer responds
- **Decides.** Which S&P notes to fight, which alternates to shoot, and whether to shoot both versions for a streaming and a broadcast cut.
- **Engine.** Content-rule table per outlet and timeslot with per-item verdicts; violations return required changes. Alternate takes create a versioning obligation in post. If S&P produces no measurable change to the shooting day or the deliverables, it is a label.
- **Takes.** 3–7 days per script
- **Television.** Broadcast and basic cable only. Streaming and premium cable have ratings advisories rather than S&P mandates; features go to the MPA ratings board after the fact.

### Script clearance report

Every name, business, phone number, licence plate, address, product, song and trademark in the script is checked against real-world use and returned with clear/change/substitute verdicts.

- **Who.** Third-party clearance company (e.g. Act One, Marshall/Plumb); Production Legal and the Script Coordinator implement
- **Decides.** Renaming characters and companies, adopting 555 numbers, whether to seek permission or substitute.
- **Engine.** Clearance object per proper noun with verdict and risk band. Changes ripple through every element containing that string, through casting breakdowns, art department signage, and prop lists. The cascade is the interesting part; model the string as an entity, not text.
- **Takes.** 5–10 business days per script
- **Television.** Series carry a running cleared-names list across seasons that new scripts are checked against.

### Product integration writing

Writing a paid brand into the story — a scene, a line, a visible use — to contract specifications about screen time, positive context and dialogue mentions.

- **Who.** Writer, at the direction of the integration/brand partnerships team
- **Decides.** Where the brand goes, whether to make it a joke, and how hard to fight a bad placement.
- **Engine.** Integration contract with deliverable terms (mentions, seconds on screen, context constraints) that must be verified against the shot script and the final cut, with revenue posting on delivery and clawback on failure.
- **Takes.** Days
- **Television.** Mostly television; features handle placement through props and marketing tie-ins rather than scripted beats.

### Budget-driven cuts

After the first budget pass, the script comes back with scenes flagged as unaffordable: night exteriors, company moves, crowd days, water, animals, children, VFX shots.

- **Who.** Line Producer and UPM raise; Showrunner/Producer decides; writer executes
- **Decides.** Combine locations, move night to day, cut the crowd, lose the sequence, or fight for it.
- **Engine.** Cost model per scene from heading fields and elements; a cut or a heading change must actually move the budget number and the schedule. INT. for EXT. changes lighting, DAY for NIGHT changes crew hours and overtime — those must be live computations, not labels.
- **Takes.** 1–2 weeks of back and forth
- **Television.** Series do this every episode against a fixed per-episode pattern budget, with 'bottle episodes' written deliberately to bank money for a big one.

### Production draft

The approved draft that goes to the departments, on white pages, with scene numbers applied and pages locked. Everything after this is a revision, not a draft.

- **Who.** Showrunner or Producer declares; Script Coordinator produces
- **Decides.** When to call it — too early and you lock a script that is still moving, too late and prep starts without a script.
- **Engine.** State transition on the script object: numbering applied, page positions frozen, revision tracking on. All departmental artefacts (breakdown, schedule, budget, storyboards, sides) now hold references to scene numbers and page numbers that must survive every later change.
- **Takes.** A day to produce; the decision is argued for weeks
- **Television.** TV locks every episode separately on a rolling weekly cadence while other episodes are still being broken, written and shot.

### Scene numbering

Assigning sequential numbers to every scene heading, printed at both margins. These numbers become the address of every element in the production for the rest of the show.

- **Who.** Script Coordinator (TV) or 1st AD/production office (film)
- **Decides.** Whether sub-headings become numbered scenes, and whether to number an intercut as one scene or two.
- **Engine.** Scene numbers are permanent identifiers, never positions. Every schedule strip, call sheet, camera report, sound report, lined script, editorial bin and continuity note keys off them. Renumbering after lock must be impossible without an explicit, expensive unlock.
- **Takes.** An hour, once
- **Television.** Multi-cam numbers scenes as letters (Scene A, Scene B) per set instead.

### Page locking and A-pages

Freezing page numbers so a change never repaginates the document. Added material becomes 25A, 25B; deleted material leaves the page number in place. Inserted scenes become A11 (US) between 10 and 11.

- **Who.** Script Coordinator
- **Decides.** Nothing is decided here — that is the point. The decision is upstream: when to lock, and whether a change is big enough to justify unlocking.
- **Engine.** Two-level lock: page lock (page identity frozen, overflow spills to lettered pages) and scene lock (numbers frozen, insertions lettered). Requires a page-position model separate from content flow. Unlocking must be a real, costly event that invalidates downstream references.
- **Takes.** Automatic once configured
- **Television.** Identical practice; TV just does it forty times a year instead of once.

### Issuing coloured revisions

Each revision set gets the next colour in the standard order, and only the changed pages (plus their A-pages) are distributed. Crews carry a script that is a stack of colours.

- **Who.** Script Coordinator issues; Production Office copies and distributes
- **Decides.** When to issue, whether a change waits for the next set, and whether to issue full colour or just pages.
- **Engine.** Revision set object {colour, date, changed element ids, pages issued}. Every crew member's script is a per-holder assembly of pages at their latest issued colour — so a crew member who missed a set is genuinely working from a wrong page, which should be able to cause an on-set error.
- **Takes.** Same-day; a set can go out in an hour
- **Television.** Series burn through colours much faster and often reset the sequence per episode; features run one continuous sequence across the whole shoot.

### Revision marks and headers

An asterisk in the right margin of every changed line, and a header on each revised page reading the colour and date, e.g. 'Blue Revised 3/14/26'. Marks are cleared when a new set starts.

- **Who.** Script Coordinator; the software
- **Decides.** Whether a change is marked (any content change is) and whether to force-mark a page for attention.
- **Engine.** Per-element revision stamp with set id; asterisks derive from stamps, not from manual marking. Clearing on new set is a bulk operation. Departments read the marks to know what to re-check — so a marked line should trigger re-verification tasks in art, props and wardrobe.
- **Takes.** Automatic
- **Television.** None.

### OMITTED handling

A cut scene keeps its number and reads 'OMITTED' — the number is retired, never reused. A page whose content is entirely gone is reissued reading OMITTED so its absence does not look like a missing page.

- **Who.** Script Coordinator
- **Decides.** Nothing; a mechanical consequence of cutting after lock.
- **Engine.** Scene state {active, omitted}; omitted scenes remain in the ordered list with number retained and content archived. Schedule strips for omitted scenes must be pulled, and any element (cast day, prop, location) whose only use was that scene must be released.
- **Takes.** Automatic
- **Television.** None.

### Script distribution

Getting the right pages to eighty people with the right permissions, individually watermarked, with confidentiality tiers — some cast see only their episode, some see only their sides.

- **Who.** Script Coordinator and Production Office; increasingly via Scenechronize, Scriptation or a studio portal
- **Decides.** Who is on the distribution list at what tier, whether to watermark, and whether to withhold the ending.
- **Engine.** Distribution ledger keyed by person, with tier, latest set received, and watermark id. Leaks trace back to a watermark id — which makes a leak a real event with a real culprit rather than a random reputational hit.
- **Takes.** 1–3 hours per set
- **Television.** High-secrecy series distribute per-scene sides only and withhold endings from the cast entirely; features do this on tentpoles.

### Sides generation

Extracting only the scenes being shot that day, at half-size, plus each actor's own scenes for audition or rehearsal. What the player-as-actor actually holds on the day.

- **Who.** 2nd AD generates from the day's schedule; Script Coordinator supplies the source
- **Decides.** As an actor: what to prepare from. As production: whether sides carry full context or just the scene.
- **Engine.** Sides derive from scene ids on the call sheet plus the current colour of each page. Audition sides are a separate extraction with the character's scenes only. Because the player-as-actor works from sides, the engine must model information the actor does not have.
- **Takes.** Automatic; minutes
- **Television.** TV actors often receive audition sides from a fake scene written specifically for casting to protect the plot.

### Shooting script assembly

The numbered, locked, colour-current script that the breakdown, schedule, budget, storyboards and shot lists are all built from. The single reference document of the production.

- **Who.** Script Coordinator; used by every department head
- **Decides.** Nothing new; it is the settled state of every prior decision.
- **Engine.** Canonical script version with scene numbers, page-eighths per scene, and typed elements exposed for the breakdown to tag (cast, extras, props, wardrobe, vehicles, animals, stunts, VFX, SFX, music, sound, set dressing, greenery, special equipment). The screenplay must be structured enough to be tagged automatically.
- **Takes.** Continuous through the shoot
- **Television.** One per episode, produced weekly, while earlier episodes are still shooting.

### On-set changes

Rewriting during shooting: an actor cannot say the line, a location fell through, the scene is not playing. Changes are made verbally, then papered as same-day revised pages.

- **Who.** Writer on set (TV: a writer is always on set; film: often nobody), Director, Showrunner by phone
- **Decides.** As the actor: whether to ask for the change. As the writer: whether to give it. As the showrunner: whether to allow it at all.
- **Engine.** On-set change request with source (actor, director, location, weather), and an approval chain that depends on the requester's power and the showrunner's protectiveness. Approved changes must generate a revision set and update sides mid-day; unpapered changes must create continuity risk.
- **Takes.** Minutes to hours
- **Television.** TV has a producer-writer on set with authority to approve; on a feature the writer is frequently not present and the director changes lines unilaterally.

### As-shot and as-broadcast script

After the cut is locked, a script reflecting exactly what was shot and then exactly what aired — used for dubbing, subtitling, music cue sheets, residuals, legal and archive.

- **Who.** Script Supervisor produces the lined/as-shot record; Post Production Coordinator produces the as-broadcast
- **Decides.** Nothing creative; it is a record, but it is the record everything downstream is paid from.
- **Engine.** Diff between shooting script and final cut at line level: lines cut, lines improvised, scenes reordered. Feeds subtitle and dub scripts, music cue sheets, and dialogue-share evidence used in credit disputes. Must be derived, not authored.
- **Takes.** Days after picture lock
- **Television.** Delivered per episode as a contractual deliverable; features deliver a combined continuity/dialogue list.

### WGA registration and copyright

Registering the script with the WGA Registry for evidentiary date-stamping, and registering copyright with the US Copyright Office for statutory damages and standing to sue.

- **Who.** Writer (father, or the player as any writer); the company for works made for hire
- **Decides.** Whether to register, at which draft, and under whose name if there are co-writers.
- **Engine.** Registration record with date and holder. Registration date is the evidence in any theft or idea-submission claim, and the presence or absence of copyright registration changes the remedies available — so a plagiarism event must resolve differently depending on it.
- **Takes.** Minutes to file; copyright processing months
- **Television.** Series material is registered by the studio as work for hire; separated rights are what the individual writer retains.

### Credit determination and arbitration

Deciding whose names go on the film and in what form. Every participating writer receives the proposed credits and all prior literary material; any protest triggers arbitration.

- **Who.** Production Executive (mother's side) proposes credits; WGA notifies participating writers; three anonymous arbiters decide if protested
- **Decides.** As producer: what to propose. As writer: whether to protest, and what to submit as your statement.
- **Engine.** Contribution ledger from element-level authorship across the version graph, scored against thresholds: on original screenplays the first writer needs >33% of the final shooting script, subsequent writers 50%; on non-original, >33%. Max three writers or two teams on screenplay credit. Arbitration must run on real word/structure shares.
- **Takes.** Notice period plus arbitration, typically 3–6 weeks (est.)
- **Television.** TV uses 'Teleplay by', 'Story by', 'Written by', 'Created by' and 'Developed by'; 'Story by' can be shared by up to three writers in TV against two in film, and Teleplay always precedes Story on the card.

### Separated rights determination

Deciding whether the writer keeps publication, dramatic stage rights, sequel payments and the right to be offered the first rewrite — the difference between selling a job and owning a piece of something.

- **Who.** WGA, on the credits it awards
- **Decides.** Nothing directly, but it is the payoff of every earlier fight over 'Story by' versus 'Teleplay by'.
- **Engine.** Rights object attached to the person, not the project: sequel and spin-off payments, 'Created by' royalties per produced episode, reacquisition rights on unproduced material. Must actually pay out on later seasons and spin-offs, or the whole credit fight is theatre.
- **Takes.** Determined with credits
- **Television.** 'Created by' on a series flows from Story by or Written by on the pilot; a writer with only Teleplay credit on the pilot gets no separated rights, which is the single most consequential credit line in television.

### Version control and archive

Keeping every draft, every revision set, the room's notes, the beat boards and the alt jokes, so that a decision made in season two can be recovered in season five.

- **Who.** Script Coordinator; Writers' Assistant maintains the room's record
- **Decides.** What to keep, what to name it, and whether to keep the versions that would embarrass someone.
- **Engine.** Directed version graph with named drafts and revision sets as nodes, element-level provenance on edges. The credit ledger, the notes history and the 'we already tried that' room memory all read this graph. Without provenance edges, arbitration and continuity are both guesswork.
- **Takes.** Continuous
- **Television.** Series carry a continuity bible across seasons: every established fact, name, date and rule, checked against every new script.

### House style enforcement

Every show has a style sheet: whether to use CUT TO:, whether to number scenes at both margins, how to write phone conversations, capitalisation of props, what act markers look like, title page layout.

- **Who.** Script Coordinator; Showrunner sets the sheet
- **Decides.** The style sheet's contents, and whether to correct a writer's draft to it silently or send it back.
- **Engine.** Style profile applied as a validation ruleset over the element list, producing violations. Two shows' profiles must produce visibly different pages from the same story. Violations cost the coordinator time and, in volume, damage a writer's reputation for professionalism.
- **Takes.** Set once, applied continuously
- **Television.** Features have looser conventions and no coordinator to enforce them until the production draft.

### Foreign language and subtitle handling

Writing dialogue in another language: the convention is to write it in English with a note that it is spoken in the language and subtitled, or to write it in-language with the translation beneath.

- **Who.** Writer, with a translator and later the localisation vendor
- **Decides.** Which convention, whether to subtitle or dub within the fiction, and how much untranslated dialogue an audience will bear.
- **Engine.** Dialogue element with language field and subtitle flag. Drives casting language requirements, dialect coach hiring, ADR planning, and localisation deliverables. Subtitled minutes are a real number some buyers cap.
- **Takes.** Days, plus translation turnaround
- **Television.** Series with recurring foreign-language characters carry a standing translator; features hire per project.

### Script timing report

A formal timing of the script — reading it aloud against a stopwatch scene by scene — to predict runtime before a frame is shot, then the cut runtime after.

- **Who.** Script Supervisor pre-shoot; Editor post-shoot
- **Decides.** Which scenes to cut before shooting them, based on a number rather than a feeling.
- **Engine.** Per-scene timing record from the read, stored against the estimate and later against the actual cut duration. The gap between the three should be a learnable, per-project bias the player can come to trust or distrust.
- **Takes.** 3–5 hours for a feature
- **Television.** Done every episode in broadcast because the slot is unforgiving; features time to a target the studio has set (often 'under two hours').

### Mini-room / development room

A small room hired before a production order to break the season and write scripts, so the studio can decide with material in hand. A defining feature of the streaming era and a fight in the 2023 MBA.

- **Who.** Showrunner plus 3+ writer-producers, pre-greenlight
- **Decides.** Whether to take a mini-room job at all, how many writers, how long, and whether scripts written there are honoured later.
- **Engine.** Room object with type {development, post-greenlight}, size, and guaranteed weeks. Under the 2023 MBA, development rooms require a minimum of 3 writer-producers guaranteed at least 10 consecutive weeks; post-greenlight rooms require 3 writers for 1–6 episodes, 5 for 7–12, 6 for 13+, for the lesser of 20 weeks or the room's duration.
- **Takes.** 10–20 weeks
- **Television.** Entirely a TV structure. Its film analogue, a writers' room on a franchise, is rare and unregulated by these minimums.

### Writers in production

Keeping writers employed while the show films, so scripts can be rewritten against what is actually happening on set — the difference between a room that dissolves at wrap and one that supports production.

- **Who.** Showrunner plus at least two other writers, during the shoot
- **Decides.** Which writers stay, whether to send a writer to set for every episode, and whether to spend the weeks on more writers for less time.
- **Engine.** Production-period staffing with the 2023 MBA rule for US/Canada single-camera streaming and pay TV: two writers besides the showrunner for the lesser of 20 weeks or the duration of production, satisfiable as combined weeks (e.g. four writers for ten weeks).
- **Takes.** Duration of principal photography
- **Television.** No film equivalent; a feature writer on set is a courtesy, not a contract.

### Option spaces

**Element types the formatter must support** — 24 options

`Scene Heading (slug)` · `Action` · `Character cue` · `Dialogue` · `Parenthetical` · `Transition` · `Shot` · `General / Text` · `Dual Dialogue pair` · `Act marker (ACT ONE / END OF ACT ONE)` · `Scene marker (multi-cam: SCENE A)` · `Cold Open / Teaser / Tag marker` · `Montage / Series of Shots block` · `Intercut block` · `New Act page break` · `Title page fields` · `Character list page (multi-cam)` · `Set list page` · `Page header (revision slug)` · `Scene number (left and right margin)` · `(MORE) and (CONT'D) continuation markers` · `(CONTINUED:) scene continueds, top and bottom` · `OMITTED marker` · `Note / Script note (non-printing)`

`Cold open + two acts + tag (half-hour comedy)` · `Cold open + three acts (half-hour, modern)` · `Two acts, no cold open (premium half-hour)` · `Teaser + four acts (classic broadcast hour)` · `Teaser + four acts + tag` · `Teaser + five acts (modern broadcast hour)` · `Five acts, no teaser` · `Six acts (basic cable, extra pods)` · `Seven acts (heavily monetised cable)` · `Six-act soap structure with a cliffhanger at each break` · `No act breaks (premium cable / streaming, act structure kept internally)` · `Two-part episode structure` · `Bottle episode` · `Musical or format-break episode` · `Multi-cam: Cold Open, Act One (scenes A–D), Act Two (scenes E–H), Tag`
