**Lock states of a script** — 8 options

`Unlocked (free repagination, free renumbering)` · `Scenes numbered, pages unlocked` · `Pages locked, scenes unlocked` · `Pages and scenes locked (production draft)` · `Locked with A-pages in circulation` · `Partially unlocked (single act relocked)` · `Full unlock and reissue (rare, expensive)` · `Frozen (no changes accepted, post-wrap)`

**Scene insertion numbering schemes** — 8 options

`US: new scene between 10 and 11 becomes A11` · `UK: new scene between 10 and 11 becomes 10A` · `Second insertion before A11 becomes AA11 (US)` · `Insertion before scene 1 becomes A1` · `Sub-numbering for setups (10A means setup A of scene 10 — do not collide with UK scene naming)` · `Page insertions: 25A, 25B, 25C` · `Deep page insertions: 25aA (Final Draft's lower-case sub-lettering)` · `Renumber all (only if unlocked)`

**Sides types** — 9 options

`Day's sides (all scenes on the call sheet, half-size)` · `Actor's personal sides (their scenes only)` · `Audition sides (from the script)` · `Audition sides (fake scene written for casting)` · `Rehearsal sides` · `Table read book (full script, distributed)` · `Director's sides with shot list` · `Sides with continuity photos attached` · `Digital sides in an app with page-turn sync`

**Script file formats and delivery** — 12 options

`.fdx (Final Draft)` · `Final Draft's revision-mode file with lock state` · `Fountain (plain text markup)` · `.celtx` · `.fadein` · `.mmsw (Movie Magic Screenwriter)` · `Watermarked PDF` · `Scenechronize / studio distribution portal` · `Scriptation (annotation-preserving PDF for departments)` · `Printed and three-hole-punched with brass brads (two brads, top and bottom holes)` · `Locked PDF with per-recipient watermark id` · `Secure viewer with screenshot blocking`

**Page-length targets by format** — 12 options

`Feature spec: 95–120 pp` · `Feature comedy: 90–105 pp` · `Feature drama/epic: 110–140 pp` · `Animated feature: 80–95 pp` · `Hour drama (broadcast): 52–62 pp` · `Hour drama (streaming, elastic): 50–75 pp` · `Half-hour single-cam: 28–34 pp` · `Half-hour multi-cam: 45–58 pp` · `Kids' 11-minute segment: 11–14 pp` · `Soap episode: 60–75 pp` · `Short film: 1–40 pp` · `Pilot (hour): 55–68 pp — pilots run long by convention`

**Which of the three the player is, and what they may touch** — 3 options

`The actor (self): reads sides, requests line changes through the writer on set, improvises at risk, sees only their own scenes on a secretive show, has no note authority unless their deal grants approvals` · `The mother (production company principal): buys material, sets and pays writing steps, gives notes with enforcement power, hires and fires writers, proposes credits, signs the WGA signatory obligations, orders budget cuts that become page cuts` · `The father (lot head, production head, and the greatest living screenwriter): writes the draft himself, does the uncredited production rewrite, showruns, breaks story, decides act-outs, locks the script, and carries the arbitration and separated-rights consequences of his own pen`

**Standards and Practices verdicts** — 10 options

`Approved as written` · `Approved with alternate required (shoot both)` · `Change mandated (specific substitution)` · `Change mandated (writer's choice of substitution)` · `Restricted to post-watershed slot` · `Requires advisory card` · `Requires disclaimer` · `Rejected — scene cannot air` · `Referred to legal (defamation / life rights)` · `Deferred to network president`

**Script clearance verdicts per proper noun** — 11 options

`Clear as written` · `Clear — no conflicting use found` · `Change required — real person or business match` · `Change required — trademark` · `Substitute with a cleared fictional name` · `Use a 555 telephone exchange` · `Use a cleared fictional area code / address` · `Permission obtainable — seek licence` · `Permission denied — must change` · `High risk — negative portrayal of a real entity` · `Requires errors and omissions insurance rider`

### What gets got wrong

- One page equals one minute is an average, not a rule, and modelling it as a constant kills the most interesting decision in the domain. A dense action page can run three minutes; a page of clipped dialogue can run forty seconds; a half-page montage runs as long as the music. The estimator must be density-aware and must return a range, because the whole cut-now-or-cut-later argument lives in the spread.
- Multi-camera page counts are roughly double single-camera for the same runtime, purely because dialogue is double-spaced and action is capitalised. A page-count model that does not switch on format will report a 22-minute sitcom as a feature.
- A-pages and A-scenes are different mechanisms and are constantly confused. A-pages (25A, 25B) absorb added material without moving page numbers. A-scenes (A11) insert scenes without renumbering. They are separate systems with separate rules and both are needed.
- US and UK scene insertion conventions are opposite. In the US, a new scene between 10 and 11 is A11, because 10A means setup A of scene 10. In the UK, the same insertion is 10A. On a co-production this is a genuine collision, not trivia.
- Revision colours are convention, not law. The white-blue-pink-yellow-green-goldenrod-buff-salmon-cherry order is the common standard and Final Draft's default, but shows deviate, and some series reset the sequence per episode. Model the sequence as configurable per production and let a deviation cause real distribution confusion.
- Revision asterisks are per changed line, not per changed page, and they clear at the start of each new revision set. Marking whole pages loses the information the art, props and wardrobe departments actually use them for.
- Locking is a one-way door with a cost. Once pages and scenes are locked, every schedule strip, breakdown row, storyboard, call sheet and editorial bin holds those numbers. An unlock is not a menu option; it is a production event that invalidates downstream artefacts.
- Cutting a scene after lock does not remove it. The number is retired and reads OMITTED, and if a whole page empties it is reissued reading OMITTED so its absence does not look like a lost page. A model that deletes the scene object breaks every reference to it.
- 'Cold open' and 'teaser' are not synonyms in trade use — a teaser is in-story hook, a cold open is often a self-contained piece before titles, and comedy and drama use the words differently. Pick one internal representation with a type field and let the display name follow the format.
- Streaming and premium series have no commercial breaks but most of them still break story in acts internally, because act-outs are how a room finds reversals. Removing act structure because there are no ads removes the tool, not the ads.
- A writer who receives only 'Teleplay by' on a pilot gets no separated rights. Separated rights flow from 'Story by' or 'Written by', and 'Created by' follows from those. This one line decides whether a person owns a piece of a show for twenty years, and it is the most consequential credit rule in television.
- Arbitration thresholds are asymmetric and easy to get wrong: on an original screenplay the first writer needs more than 33% of the final shooting script but a subsequent writer needs 50%; on a non-original, everyone needs more than 33%. Screenplay credit maxes at three writers or two teams. '&' means a team, 'and' means separate passes.
- The showrunner rewrites every episode as a matter of routine and usually does not take credit; the same volume of rewriting on a feature would trigger arbitration and could take the original writer's credit away. Credit generosity is a cultural difference between the two media, not a rules difference, and the engine should let it be violated.
- Free rewrites are prohibited under the WGA MBA, which means the pressure to do them is the interesting thing to model, along with the consequences when a signatory company is caught. A model that just charges a fee per step misses the entire dynamic.
- A slug line is not a string. It is a set of fields that create a set, a lighting condition, a crew-hours regime, a permit type and a budget line. If changing NIGHT to DAY does not move the budget and the schedule, the heading is decoration.
- Sides are the actor's epistemic boundary. If the player-as-actor can read the whole script whenever they like, the most distinctive information asymmetry in the domain is gone. Withhold at the data layer, not in the prose.
- The 2023 MBA staffing and span numbers apply only to seasons whose first episode was written after 1 December 2023 — pre-existing series are grandfathered. A simulation spanning that date needs the rule to switch on by cohort, not globally.
- Page count is not the same currency as page eighths. Screenplays are measured in pages for runtime and in eighths of a page for scheduling, and an eighth is a physical strip of page, not an eighth of a minute. Keep both, derived from the same pagination model.
- Scene numbers are addresses, not positions. The moment anything sorts by scene number to get story order, A11 and 10A break it. Store order separately from identity.
- The dialogue column is roughly 35 characters wide in Courier 12 at standard margins, which is why a speech's page cost is not proportional to its word count in any simple way. Character-level line wrapping per element type is the only way page counts come out right.

---

## 3. Genre, format, rating and everything a picture IS

*the identity layer: what shape the work takes, what kind of thing it is, who it is allowed to be shown to, and how long it runs, in film and in television, worldwide.*

### Format determination

Buyer and seller settle whether the material is a feature, a short, a returning series, a limited series, an anthology, a TV movie, a special or short-form, before a word is commissioned.

- **Who.** President of Production / Head of Studio (father); President of the production company (mother); Head of Drama or Head of Comedy at the buyer
- **Decides.** Which shape the work takes, which buyer it can go to, whether the deal is a feature option or a series commitment, and which guild agreement and residual formula attach.
- **Engine.** Title.format as an enum that gates six tables at once: script-length rule, schedule generator, guild agreement, classification body, awards path, window ladder. Changing format must reroute all six, not relabel one. Sweep: flip format, the shortlist of buyers must change.
- **Takes.** One 45-minute meeting to six months of shape-shopping
- **Television.** A feature is one negative cost. A series is an order with an option ladder, a pattern (recurring) budget and a per-episode budget, and the format decision also fixes episode count, slot length and act count.

### Genre and subgenre declaration

The picture is assigned a primary genre and up to three subgenres. This is not a label: it sets audience expectation, department load, marketable elements and which territories will pre-buy.

- **Who.** Screenwriter (father); Development Executive; Head of Development at the production company (mother)
- **Decides.** Primary genre, subgenres, hybrid tags, and whether the picture is sold as the thing it is or against type.
- **Engine.** Genre record holding: setpiece cadence (beats/100 pages), night-exterior ratio, stunt and SFX day load, VFX shot count band, star-dependence coefficient, per-territory presale multiplier, ratings pressure, awards seasonality. Falsifiable: change genre, budget shape and presales must both move.
- **Takes.** Fixed at pitch; contested for the life of the project
- **Television.** Television genre is also a slot genre: procedural, soap, sitcom and panel show are scheduling objects with fixed strip positions. A feature's genre is a marketing position; a series' genre is a timetable.

### Tone and register setting

Within a genre, the register is fixed: straight, heightened, comic, satirical, camp, grim, elevated, absurd. A horror picture at 'elevated' and the same picture at 'gory fun' are different products with different buyers.

- **Who.** Screenwriter (father); Showrunner; Director
- **Decides.** Which audience the marketing addresses, whether critics or opening weekend are the target, and how far the rating can be pushed.
- **Engine.** A register axis separate from genre, read by: critic-population response curve, audience-population response curve, festival acceptance odds, per-territory taste weights. Two axes must diverge — one scalar wearing two names fails the test.
- **Takes.** Set in the first draft; re-set at every rewrite
- **Television.** A series register drifts across a season and across seasons, and the drift is measurable. A feature's register is fixed at lock.

### Logline and one-sheet lock

A 25-50 word statement of the picture with protagonist, want, obstacle and irony. Locked because it is the object every buyer, agent and financier actually reads.

- **Who.** Development Executive; Head of Marketing; Screenwriter (father)
- **Decides.** The one sentence used in every pitch, and (at the Emmys, mandatorily) the 50-word log line filed with a limited-or-anthology-series or movie entry.
- **Engine.** A generated logline from the picture's own structured facts (protagonist archetype, want, antagonist class, setting, irony), scored for legibility. NPC readers parse it, not a random draw. Change the antagonist class, the logline and the read both change.
- **Takes.** Hours to weeks; rewritten dozens of times
- **Television.** Television additionally needs a series engine statement — what generates a story every week — which a feature does not have and cannot fake.

### Comparable titles (comps) analysis

Three to eight released pictures of like genre, budget band, rating and star tier are pulled, and their actual performance is used to price this one.

- **Who.** Head of Development (mother); Finance Executive; Sales Agent
- **Decides.** The budget the picture is allowed, the presale estimates, the P&A commitment, and whether a financier will close.
- **Engine.** Query over the library of released titles in the save: filter by genre, subgenre, rating band, budget band, star tier, release corridor; return real recorded grosses and windows. Estimates are regressions on that table. A comp set with three titles must price worse than one with eight.
- **Takes.** 2-5 days per financing pass
- **Television.** Television comps are per-platform and per-slot: a show is comped against what ran in that hour on that network, plus completion rate on streaming, not against box office.

### Quadrant and demo targeting

The target audience is fixed as quadrants (male/female × under/over 25) for film, or a sales demo (18-49, 25-54, 2-11, 6-11, 12-17) for television.

- **Who.** President of Marketing; Head of Research; Network Scheduler
- **Decides.** Rating ceiling, casting brief, marketing spend split, and for television which advertisers can be sold against the show.
- **Engine.** Audience population segmented on the existing geography and demography, with per-segment genre affinity and per-segment rating admissibility. Targeting a quadrant must change both the cast shortlist and the achievable rating band, or it is decoration.
- **Takes.** Set at greenlight; re-tested after the first screening
- **Television.** Broadcast television is sold on a demo guarantee to advertisers with make-goods if the demo misses. Streaming targets completion and retention instead, and has no make-good.

### Rating covenant negotiation

The distribution or financing agreement states a required classification — commonly 'no more restrictive than PG-13' in the US, or a required BBFC band for a UK broadcaster. Delivering outside it is a breach.

- **Who.** Business Affairs Executive; Studio Head (father); Distributor
- **Decides.** The hard ceiling every other creative decision has to live under, and who pays to recut if it is missed.
- **Engine.** Title.ratingCovenant {body, maxBand, curePeriodDays, breachRemedy}. A delivered classification worse than the covenant fires a real contractual consequence against the ledger: recut cost, holdback, or loss of the minimum guarantee.
- **Takes.** Negotiated over 2-8 weeks with the distribution deal
- **Television.** Television's equivalent is a standards covenant plus a time-slot guarantee: the show must be deliverable at the hour it was bought for, which is stricter than a single band.

### Rating-risk pass on the script

The script is read against the target band and every element that could move it is flagged: language counts and context, nudity, drug use, sexual content, violence intensity, and depiction of smoking.

- **Who.** Production Counsel; a CARA-experienced ratings consultant; Line Producer
- **Decides.** Which lines and beats are written in two versions, what is shot as coverage for a softer cut, and what is simply cut before it is paid for.
- **Engine.** A content ledger per scene: tagged elements with intensity and context (sexual vs non-sexual use of the same word, drug use rewarded vs punished, smoking by a minor). Band prediction reads the ledger. Remove one tagged element, the predicted band must be able to move.
- **Takes.** 3-10 days per draft
- **Television.** On television this pass is Standards and Practices and it happens on every episode, every week, against a house standard rather than an outside board.

### CARA submission and rating assignment

The finished picture is screened for CARA's board of parent raters, who assign G, PG, PG-13, R or NC-17 plus a descriptor naming why. Notice follows within about 24 hours; the certificate in roughly four business days.

- **Who.** Distributor's Ratings Liaison; Classification and Rating Administration (CARA) Rating Board; the filmmaker attends nothing
- **Decides.** Nothing the filmmaker controls at this point — only whether to accept, recut and resubmit, appeal, or release unrated.
- **Engine.** A rater panel as a real population: each rater has a child-age band and per-element sensitivity, and the band is the panel's median, not a formula. Same picture, different panel draw, occasionally a different band — and the audit record says so honestly.
- **Takes.** Notice ~24 hours after screening; certificate ~4 business days; slot booked 1-3 weeks ahead
- **Television.** There is no CARA for television. A US television programme is self-rated by the network or producer under the TV Parental Guidelines; only complaints reach the Monitoring Board.

### Recut and re-submission

Frames are trimmed, a word is looped, a shot is reframed, and the picture goes back for another screening at a new fee. Repeat until the band lands or the money runs out.

- **Who.** Editor; Director; Distributor's Ratings Liaison
- **Decides.** How many passes to buy, what is sacrificed, and whether the director's cut survives as a separate version.
- **Engine.** Each resubmission costs money and calendar days against the release date, and edits the content ledger. The band recomputes from the edited ledger. Three resubmissions must be able to cost the release slot.
- **Takes.** 5-15 days per pass, editorial plus screening
- **Television.** The television equivalent is an S&P note cycle inside the post schedule — same mechanic, no external fee, and a hard airdate rather than a movable one.

### CARA appeal

The distributor argues the assigned rating is clearly erroneous. Overturning requires a two-thirds majority of the Appeals Board. Comparable released pictures may be cited.

- **Who.** Distributor's senior executive argues; CARA Appeals Board of exhibition and distribution people hears it
- **Decides.** Whether to spend the time and the political capital, and whether to go in with an argument or with a recut already in hand.
- **Engine.** An appeal is a vote of a modelled board with known dispositions and a two-thirds threshold, reading the picture's content ledger and its cited comps. It must be able to uphold — a costly appeal that always wins is a slot machine.
- **Takes.** 2-6 weeks from filing to hearing to decision
- **Television.** No equivalent. A television dispute is settled with the network, not an industry board.

### Advertising and trailer band approval

Every trailer, teaser, poster, still and TV spot is separately approved. Green band plays with all audiences, red band only before R/NC-17/unrated features, yellow band is for age-gated internet placement.

- **Who.** Head of Creative Advertising; CARA advertising staff
- **Decides.** Where the campaign can run, whether a red-band cut exists at all, and what the poster is allowed to show.
- **Engine.** Materials are separate objects with their own band and their own placement rules. A red-band-only campaign reaches a smaller modelled audience than a green-band one, and that reach difference must show up in opening-weekend awareness.
- **Takes.** 3-10 business days per piece of material
- **Television.** Television promos are cleared by the network's own S&P against the slot the promo will run in, not against the show's own rating.

### BBFC submission and pre-submission advice

The work is submitted for U, PG, 12A, 12, 15, 18 or R18. Paid pre-submission advice on a rough cut will name the cuts required for a target category before the money is spent on a finish.

- **Who.** UK distributor; BBFC Compliance Officers (two view; contentious titles escalate)
- **Decides.** Whether to take the category offered or make the named cuts to reach a lower one; and whether to buy advice early or gamble late.
- **Engine.** Advice is a purchasable, dated service returning a specific cut list from the content ledger, cheaper before the lock than after. Buying it early must genuinely reduce the cost of hitting the band.
- **Takes.** Advice 1-3 weeks; formal classification typically 1-3 weeks, longer for cut negotiation
- **Television.** UK television is not classified at all. It is scheduled against the Ofcom watershed instead, and the same programme can be compliant at 22:00 and a breach at 19:00.

### Territory classification submissions

Every territory the picture is sold into classifies it separately, under its own bands, its own fee and its own turnaround — and some can refuse it outright.

- **Who.** International Sales Agent; each local distributor; each territory's board
- **Decides.** Which territories get which version, which are worth the submission cost, and which are abandoned because the required cuts are unacceptable.
- **Engine.** A per-territory classification record {body, band, fee, turnaroundDays, cutsRequired, refusedFlag} with each body reading the SAME content ledger through DIFFERENT rules. A picture must be able to be 15 in the UK and 18 in Germany from one ledger.
- **Takes.** 1-8 weeks per territory; China's chain runs ~30 working days provincial review, 3-5 for the licence, 10-15 technical
- **Television.** Television is classified per-territory too, but usually by the licensing broadcaster's own compliance department against its slot, not by a national board.

### Territory version and cut-list management

Each territory's required cuts and each airline, broadcast and platform version are conformed, re-graded, re-mixed and re-delivered as separate masters with separate QC.

- **Who.** Post Supervisor; Distributor's Technical Services; the local distributor's compliance staff
- **Decides.** How many versions exist, who pays for each, and which one is considered the picture.
- **Engine.** Versions are first-class objects with their own runtime, content ledger, band and delivery cost. Ten versions cost ten times the QC. A version's ledger must be able to differ from its parent's and classify differently.
- **Takes.** 2-10 days of conform, mix and QC per version
- **Television.** Television generates more versions per hour of content than film does — broadcast, streaming, international, airline, in-flight-safe, and a clean-text version with no burned titles for dubbing territories.

### TV self-rating and descriptor assignment

The programme is assigned TV-Y, TV-Y7, TV-G, TV-PG, TV-14 or TV-MA, with content descriptors D, L, S, V or (only with TV-Y7) FV, and encoded for the V-chip.

- **Who.** Network Standards and Practices Director; the producer for a first-run syndicated show; TV Parental Guidelines Monitoring Board only on complaint
- **Decides.** Which hour the show can occupy, which advertisers will buy against it, and whether parental blocking suppresses part of the audience.
- **Engine.** The rating is set by the broadcaster the player can lobby, not an external board — so it is a negotiation with a named person, with a relationship cost. The band then feeds slot eligibility and advertiser willingness, both of which must move with it.
- **Takes.** Per episode, inside the S&P pass; minutes once the standard is known
- **Television.** This process has no film equivalent: the seller rates its own show. The film-side analogue, CARA, is deliberately outside the production's reach.

### Standards and Practices notes

Every script and every cut is read against the network's own standards: language, product depiction, brand disparagement, imitable acts, medical accuracy, depiction of suicide and self-harm, sponsor conflicts.

- **Who.** Vice President, Broadcast Standards and Practices; Showrunner; Line Producer
- **Decides.** What is rewritten, what is shot two ways, what is blurred or looped in post, and whether a fight with S&P is worth having.
- **Engine.** S&P is a person with a standard, a memory and a temperature, not a filter function. Winning a note costs relationship capital that is spent from a finite balance and read by later requests. Push ten times and the eleventh fails.
- **Takes.** 24-72 hours per script; same-day on a cut
- **Television.** Film has no S&P. Its nearest equivalents are the distributor's marketing restrictions and the ratings covenant, both of which are contractual rather than editorial.

### Watershed and time-zone scheduling

The programme is placed in a slot its classification permits: the UK 21:00 watershed (20:00 on unprotected premium film services), Australian G/PG/M/MA15+ time zones, German 20:00/22:00/23:00 bands, Dutch 20:00 and midnight bands.

- **Who.** Network Scheduler; Compliance Manager; Head of Programming
- **Decides.** The hour, the competing programmes, the reachable audience, and whether a cut is needed to move earlier.
- **Engine.** Slot eligibility is a function of band × territory × clock, and the reachable audience is a function of the slot on real population and real local time — machinery the world model already has. Move a show an hour, the audience must move.
- **Takes.** Scheduled 6-16 weeks ahead; changed in a week under pressure
- **Television.** This is television-only. The film analogue is showtime placement and the number of shows a runtime allows per screen per day.

### Series bible and format document

A document fixing the premise, the engine that generates episodes, the series' rules, the regular cast, the world, the arc plan and the tone — the thing the network buys and every subsequent writer is held to.

- **Who.** Showrunner (Executive Producer); Creator; Head of Development (mother)
- **Decides.** How many stories the show can tell before it repeats, which characters are regular versus recurring versus guest, and what the show is not.
- **Engine.** A story-generation record: story engine class, conflict sources, character relationship matrix, setting inventory, and a repetition counter that degrades audience response when the same generator fires too often. A thin bible must run dry sooner.
- **Takes.** 3-8 weeks to write; revised every season
- **Television.** Film has no bible. Its nearest equivalent is the treatment, which is a sales document rather than a rulebook, and nothing later is held against it.

### Series order and option ladder negotiation

The order is fixed: number of episodes, per-episode licence fee, pattern budget, the buyer's options on later seasons, exclusivity and the number of years talent is held.

- **Who.** Business Affairs at the buyer; the production company's President (mother); talent agency
- **Decides.** Episode count, money per episode, how many seasons the buyer can compel, and how long the cast is locked out of other work.
- **Engine.** An option ladder as dated objects the OTHER side holds — exercise windows, escalators, and a bonus if exercised early. The player's actor can be held by an option they cannot break. Change the option count, the number of offers the actor can accept must change.
- **Takes.** 2-10 weeks; exercised or lapsed per season on dated windows
- **Television.** A feature actor's deal ends at wrap plus press days. A series regular's deal is typically a six- or seven-year option held by the studio, which is the defining difference between a film career and a television one.

### Season arc break

The season is broken on the board into episodes: arcs plotted, act-outs placed, episode premises assigned, and the order of episodes locked well enough to script out of order.

- **Who.** Showrunner; the writers' room (Co-EP, Supervising Producer, Story Editor, Staff Writers); Writers' Assistant on the board
- **Decides.** What happens in which episode, which writer gets which script, where the midseason turn and the finale cliffhanger land.
- **Engine.** Episodes are objects with arc position, premise, assigned writer and dependency on prior episodes. Break order versus shoot order must be able to diverge, and an out-of-order shoot must create real continuity risk the Script Supervisor tracks.
- **Takes.** 2-6 weeks of room time before scripts start
- **Television.** A feature has no room and no arc board; it has a beat sheet and one writer, or a succession of writers who never meet.

### Act structure and act-break design

The number and placement of acts is fixed by where the commercials go: US network hour at teaser plus five or six acts, network half-hour at cold open, two acts and a tag, cable hour at four acts, premium and streaming at none.

- **Who.** Screenwriter (father); Showrunner; Network Executive
- **Decides.** Where every episode's cliffhangers sit, how many of them there are, and whether the writer is writing to a hook every eleven minutes or to nothing.
- **Engine.** Act count as a structural constraint the script generator must satisfy, with an act-out strength per break read by the audience-retention model. Move a show from six acts to none and both the script shape and the retention curve must change.
- **Takes.** Fixed at format; re-fixed if the show moves platform
- **Television.** This is the clearest case where television is not film with more episodes. An ad-supported hour is engineered around five or six hooks; a streaming hour is engineered around one.

### Runtime target and timing to slot

The cut is timed to the buyer's exact total running time, frequently to the frame, with the ad load subtracted from the slot. Over-length means cutting; under-length means shooting or stretching.

- **Who.** Post Producer; Editor; Network Standards and Delivery
- **Decides.** What comes out of a finished cut for arithmetic rather than craft, and what the delivered TRT actually is.
- **Engine.** Slot minus ad load equals permitted TRT, per buyer and per territory, with a tolerance. A cut outside tolerance is rejected at delivery and costs days. The ad load is a property of the buyer, so selling the same show elsewhere changes the cut.
- **Takes.** Hours to days per episode at the end of picture editorial
- **Television.** Film has no slot, only commercial pressure: under two hours buys an extra daily showtime per screen, which is real money and is the only arithmetic on a feature's runtime.

### Pilot, presentation or direct-to-series decision

The buyer orders a full produced pilot, a cheaper presentation (a partial pilot, often 12-20 minutes, unaired), or skips both and orders the series straight off the script and the package.

- **Who.** Head of Drama or Comedy at the buyer; Studio Head (father); Showrunner
- **Decides.** How much is spent before the show exists, how much evidence the pickup decision has, and how much of the cast is locked before anyone has seen a frame.
- **Engine.** Three distinct spend-and-information paths: pilot costs most and yields the most signal; presentation costs less and yields less; direct-to-series costs nothing up front and yields none, so its failure rate must genuinely be higher.
- **Takes.** Pilot 4-8 weeks prep-to-cut; presentation 1-3 weeks; direct-to-series instant
- **Television.** Entirely television. Its film cousin is the proof-of-concept short, which buys attachment rather than a pickup.

### Backdoor pilot and planted spinoff

A new show's pilot is produced as an episode of an existing series, so it airs and earns whether or not it goes to series. Or characters are planted in the parent show to test audience response.

- **Who.** Showrunner of the parent series; Head of Current Programming; Studio Head (father)
- **Decides.** Whether a spinoff gets tested at someone else's cost, which regulars cross over, and whether the parent's audience is spent on it.
- **Engine.** An episode can carry a second title's identity. Audience response to the planted characters is measured separately from the episode's, and the spinoff pickup decision reads that separated number. If the split is not measured, the mechanic is a label.
- **Takes.** One episode slot in an existing season
- **Television.** Television-only. Film's equivalent is the post-credit scene and the shared-universe plant, which tests nothing and commits more.

### Pilot testing and retooling

The pilot is screened for recruited audiences with dial testing and questionnaires. Weak scores produce recasting, a new pilot director, reshoots, or a wholesale retool before the series order.

- **Who.** Head of Research at the network; testing vendor; Showrunner
- **Decides.** Whether it goes to series, who gets recast between pilot and episode two, and whether the premise survives.
- **Engine.** A test is a sample of the real audience population with sampling error, per-segment scores and a known relationship to actual performance that is good but not perfect. Recasting from a test must sometimes be the wrong call, and the audit must record the true value.
- **Takes.** Testing 1-2 weeks; retool 2-8 weeks
- **Television.** Features test too, with recruited screenings and score cards, but a feature reshoots an ending. A pilot recasts a person — which is where the player's actor gets fired for reasons that are not about them.

### The pitch

A 10-30 minute verbal pitch with logline, engine, characters, first season and where it goes, delivered in a room, usually to several buyers in a week.

- **Who.** Creator/Screenwriter (father); Producer (mother); the buyer's Head of Drama, Head of Comedy or Head of Unscripted
- **Decides.** Who buys, at what commitment level (script, script plus penalty, pilot, series), and on what terms.
- **Engine.** Buyer objects with slate needs, current holes, genre appetite, existing overlap and a budget. A pitch is scored against THAT buyer's needs, so pitching the same show to five buyers must yield five different answers.
- **Takes.** 30 minutes in the room; a 2-4 week circuit; weeks to an answer
- **Television.** Feature pitching sells a one-off; television pitching sells a business that runs for years, so the buyer is also buying the showrunner's capacity to run it, which is assessed separately from the material.

### Documentary mode and access agreement

The film's mode is chosen — observational, expository, participatory, poetic, reflexive or performative — and access to subjects and archive is negotiated with the control each side gets over the result.

- **Who.** Documentary Director; Producer (mother); Commissioning Editor; subjects' counsel
- **Decides.** Whether there is narration, whether the filmmaker appears, how much archive is needed, and what the subject can veto.
- **Engine.** Mode sets shooting ratio (observational runs 50:1 to 200:1 and more), crew size, schedule length, archive licence budget and post length. Access terms are contract objects with veto rights that can actually remove finished material.
- **Takes.** Access 1-12 months to negotiate; shoot 6 months to 10 years
- **Television.** Documentary series carry a fixed episode count and delivery dates against an open-ended subject, which is the central tension of the form; a feature doc can wait for its ending.

### Unscripted format design

A paper format is written: the game or process, the rules, the stages, the jeopardy, the elimination mechanic, the set, the run of play, and the 'format point' that makes it unlike anything else.

- **Who.** Format Creator; Head of Unscripted Development; Executive Producer
- **Decides.** Whether the show is a format that can be sold abroad or a one-territory production, and how much of the value survives changing the cast.
- **Engine.** The format is an executable rule set with stage counts, elimination arithmetic and per-episode outcome generation. Contestants are people. If the format cannot be simulated to produce a winner from real contestant attributes, the show is a label.
- **Takes.** 2-8 weeks to write; 1-2 days for a tabletop run-through
- **Television.** Unscripted has no film equivalent. Its economics are inverted: the format, not the cast, is the asset, and the cast is replaced every season by design.

### Format rights licensing and local adaptation

The format is licensed into a territory with a production bible, a consultant, and rules that must be honoured. A flying producer from the rights holder oversees the local version.

- **Who.** Head of Format Sales at the distributor; local Executive Producer; Flying Producer
- **Decides.** Which territories take it, what the licence fee and per-episode royalty are, and how much local deviation is permitted.
- **Engine.** Format licence objects with per-territory fee, royalty, term, exclusivity and a fidelity score. Local versions succeed as a function of fidelity × local taste weights. An idea alone is not protectable — what sells is the bible plus a ratings record.
- **Takes.** 3-12 months from option to local premiere
- **Television.** Scripted formats are remade too (a script licence plus local rewrite), but the unscripted format trade is larger and the bible, not the script, is the licensed object.

### Animation technique and pipeline commitment

The technique is locked: 2D hand-drawn, 2D digital, cut-out, 3D CG, stop-motion (puppet, clay, object, pixilation, cut-paper), rotoscope, motion capture, hybrid or experimental.

- **Who.** Animation Director; Head of Studio (father); Producer; Pipeline Supervisor
- **Decides.** Crew composition, schedule length, cost per finished minute, whether a shot can be changed after it is animated, and where the work can be done.
- **Engine.** Per-technique cost and rate per finished minute, crew mix, and a change-cost curve that rises steeply once animation starts. Stop-motion shot in real time cannot be re-timed in post; CG can. Change technique, the schedule must change shape, not just length.
- **Takes.** 2D/3D series episode 6-9 months in pipeline; stop-motion feature 3-5 years
- **Television.** Animated series run overlapping episode pipelines with a rolling delivery rather than a single finish, so the crew is sized to throughput per week rather than to a shoot.

### Production committee formation and cour ordering

An anime is financed by a production committee, each member taking a window of rights, and the order is placed in cours — three-month broadcast quarters of roughly 10-13 weekly episodes.

- **Who.** Anime Producer; committee members (publisher, broadcaster, music label, toy company, streamer); Studio Head
- **Decides.** Who owns which rights, how many cours are ordered, which quarter it airs in, and what the merchandise and music plans are.
- **Engine.** A committee is a cap table with per-member rights windows, so revenue splits by window and member rather than by a single waterfall. Cour is a scheduling unit tied to the real calendar quarters the world model already keeps.
- **Takes.** Committee formed 12-24 months before broadcast; a cour is 3 months on air
- **Television.** Japanese scheduling is quarterly and near-universal: a single cour is 12-13 episodes, a double cour 24-26 across two contiguous quarters. Western season lengths do not map onto it.

### Strip production line design

A soap or telenovela is built as a factory: scripts written months ahead, scenes shot out of order by set rather than by episode, several directors in rotation, and five episodes a week delivered indefinitely or to a fixed chapter count.

- **Who.** Executive Producer of the serial; Production Manager; Block Director; Storyline Producer
- **Decides.** How many episodes per week, how many units, how far ahead the story is written, and how quickly a cast departure can be written out.
- **Engine.** Block scheduling that groups scenes by standing set across multiple episodes, producing a real efficiency gain over per-episode scheduling and a real continuity risk. Lead time between script and air is a number that constrains how fast story can react.
- **Takes.** Telenovela 120-200 chapters; a daytime soap ~250-260 episodes a year
- **Television.** A telenovela ends on a planned chapter count and releases its cast; a soap is open-ended and never does. They look alike and are different businesses.

### Multi-camera week

Monday table read, notes and rewrite; Tuesday and Wednesday blocking and run-throughs with network and studio runs; Thursday camera blocking and pre-shoots; Friday tape night before a live audience, shot in sequence, two takes of most scenes.

- **Who.** Director; Showrunner; Stage Manager; Audience Warm-up; four camera operators on pedestals
- **Decides.** How the week is spent, what gets rewritten after which run-through, what is pre-shot, and whether the audience laugh is real.
- **Engine.** A five-day per-episode cycle with named days, each with its own deliverable, and a joke-performance model where the audience response is measured and feeds the overnight rewrite. Not a generic shoot day with a different label.
- **Takes.** 5 days per episode; tape night 3-5 hours
- **Television.** This has no film equivalent whatsoever. It is the one production model where the audience is physically present and the script changes in response to them the same night.

### Single-camera schedule model

Shot like a small feature: location and stage days, coverage-based, no audience, typically 5-8 days per half-hour episode and 7-10 per hour, often with overlapping units on a series.

- **Who.** Line Producer; First Assistant Director; Director
- **Decides.** Days per episode, unit count, location days versus stage days, and how many episodes a director gets.
- **Engine.** Days-per-episode as a computed number from page count, setup count, location moves and company moves — not a constant. Shortening the schedule must degrade something measurable, not just cost less.
- **Takes.** Half-hour 5-8 days; hour 7-10 days; premium dramas run longer
- **Television.** The model is film's, but the cadence is not: a series shoots the next episode while the last is in post and the one after that is being written, with three departments running on different episodes at once.

### Procedural versus serialized design

The show is designed as case-of-the-week (self-contained, viewable in any order), fully serialized, or hybrid with a light season arc under closed episodes.

- **Who.** Showrunner; Head of Current Programming; Head of Distribution
- **Decides.** Whether the show is worth anything in syndication and repeats, how punishing a missed episode is, and how quickly the writers' room burns through premise.
- **Engine.** Episode independence as a real number driving repeat and library value, and serialization driving a retention penalty per missed episode. A procedural must actually be worth more in library and less in binge, or the axis is decorative.
- **Takes.** Decided in the bible; drifts over seasons
- **Television.** This axis does not exist for film. It is the single largest determinant of a series' back-end value and of how much of a hit the show must be to survive.

### Anthology reset design

The season or the episode resets: new story, new characters, frequently a new cast, under a continuing title. Season-anthology (new story each season) and episode-anthology (new story each episode) are different productions.

- **Who.** Creator; Showrunner; Head of Casting
- **Decides.** Whether stars can be booked for one season only, whether sets and crew carry over, and which awards category the show can enter.
- **Engine.** A title whose seasons or episodes hold independent casts, budgets and story states. Casting cost must rise (new leads every unit) while continuity cost falls (no returning sets or arcs). Both effects must exist, not one.
- **Takes.** Per season or per episode
- **Television.** Film's portmanteau feature is the closest relative and is rare. On television the anthology is also an awards strategy: an episode-anthology enters as an anthology series, a season-anthology enters as a limited series.

### Title registration and clearance

A title is registered with the MPA's Title Registration Bureau among subscriber companies, and separately cleared against trademarks and confusable existing titles. Disputes go to arbitration between subscribers.

- **Who.** Legal Affairs; the MPA Title Registration Bureau; a clearance service
- **Decides.** Whether a picture keeps its title, and what a dispute costs to settle or to lose.
- **Engine.** A registry of titles in the save with priority by registration date and confusability scoring, generating real disputes between companies. If two pictures can share a name with no consequence, the registry is ornamental.
- **Takes.** Registration days; a dispute weeks to months
- **Television.** Series titles are registered the same way, and the Emmys separately require a limited-or-anthology series' main title to be distinct from any parent series it split off from.

### Underlying rights classification

The picture is classified as original, adaptation (novel, play, article, podcast, game, comic), remake, sequel, prequel, reboot, spinoff, based on a true story, or public domain — each with a different rights chain.

- **Who.** Business Affairs; Literary Agent; Production Counsel
- **Decides.** What must be bought, from whom, for how long, in which media and territories, and what the writer's credit can be.
- **Engine.** Chain of title as linked rights objects with grantor, scope, term, reversion date and holdbacks, gating the capability to shoot. An expired or incomplete chain must block the greenlight the way the existing capability gate already blocks uncovered shooting.
- **Takes.** Option 2-12 weeks; a contested chain, years
- **Television.** A series option must also carry sequel and series rights and a per-episode royalty to the underlying author, which a one-off feature option need not.

### Life rights and true-story clearance

Living subjects are paid for cooperation and for a release; the depiction of everyone else is cleared against defamation and privacy risk, with a written annotation tying every depicted fact to a source.

- **Who.** Production Counsel; Clearance Attorney; Producer (mother)
- **Decides.** Who cooperates, who is composited or renamed, what disclaimers appear, and whether the picture is insurable at all.
- **Engine.** An annotated-script object mapping each depicted claim to a source and a risk grade, which E&O insurance prices from and can refuse. Distribution should be gated on E&O the way shooting is already gated on cover.
- **Takes.** Annotation 3-8 weeks; life rights deals 1-9 months
- **Television.** Series in this space re-clear every episode, and a subject's cooperation can be withdrawn mid-season, which a delivered feature cannot suffer.

### Nationality and cultural certification

The picture is certified as a national work — by cultural points test, by treaty co-production, or by official co-production status — which is what unlocks the incentive and, in some territories, the quota status.

- **Who.** Head of Physical Production; production accountant; the certifying body (BFI, CNC, CAVCO, Screen Australia)
- **Decides.** Where the picture can shoot, who must be hired in which roles, what proportion of spend must be local, and how much cash comes back.
- **Engine.** A points test evaluated against the actual cast, crew nationalities, shooting locations and post locations already in the save, wired to the existing incentive regimes. Recast one head of department and the points total must be able to fall below the pass mark.
- **Takes.** Interim certificate at prep; final after delivery, 3-12 months
- **Television.** High-end television is certified under parallel rules with a per-hour spend threshold rather than a per-picture one, so a series can qualify while a single episode could not.

### Quota qualification

The work is counted or not counted toward a territory's obligations: European works on a VOD catalogue, Canadian-content points, an Australian drama sub-quota, a local-language requirement.

- **Who.** Head of Compliance at the broadcaster or platform; Commissioning Editor; Regulator
- **Decides.** Whether a buyer needs this specific show to meet a legal obligation — which is the strongest bargaining position a seller can have.
- **Engine.** Buyers hold live quota positions (hours short, deadline), and a show that fills a shortfall commands a premium the same show does not command at the start of the year. Buyer demand must move with the buyer's own deficit.
- **Takes.** Assessed annually; reported to the regulator
- **Television.** Quotas are overwhelmingly a television and on-demand instrument; theatrical quotas exist but are screen-time rules on exhibitors rather than commissioning obligations.

### Presentation format selection

The picture commits to 2D, stereoscopic 3D, large-format (IMAX film or digital, with the expanded 1.43:1 or 1.90:1 frame), high frame rate, HDR, immersive audio (Dolby Atmos, DTS:X, Auro), premium large format, or 4DX/ScreenX enhancement.

- **Who.** Director; Director of Photography; Studio Head (father); Head of Distribution
- **Decides.** Screens available, ticket price premium, camera and lens package, on-set frame-line management, and post cost and length.
- **Engine.** Per-format screen inventory, premium per ticket, and added negative and post cost. The count of large-format screens is finite and booked, so two tentpoles in one corridor must be able to collide over them.
- **Takes.** Decided at prep; IMAX certification in post adds 2-6 weeks
- **Television.** Television's equivalents are HD/UHD, HDR grade and Atmos mix, decided by the platform's delivery specification rather than chosen for a premium, and they change post cost without changing the audience.

### Aspect ratio and frame rate declaration

The shooting and finishing ratios are fixed (1.33, 1.66, 1.78, 1.85, 2.00, 2.39, 1.43 IMAX, or varying ratios within one picture) along with frame rate and whether the capture is spherical, anamorphic or large-format.

- **Who.** Director of Photography; Director; Post Supervisor
- **Decides.** Lens package, framing charts, what is protected in the frame for other ratios, and which deliverables need separate versions.
- **Engine.** Ratio and rate as properties of the shot that constrain the lens inventory available and the reframing possible in post. A picture finished at 2.39 must cost something to deliver at 1.78, not be free.
- **Takes.** Fixed in prep over 1-2 weeks of camera tests
- **Television.** Television is effectively locked to 1.78 by platform specification, with rare exceptions, so the choice that is creative on a feature is a compliance item on a series.

### Version management

Distinct authored versions are created and tracked: theatrical cut, international cut, director's cut, extended edition, unrated, airline edit, broadcast edit, platform edit, and the clean-text master for dubbing.

- **Who.** Post Supervisor; Editor; Distributor's Home Entertainment executive
- **Decides.** Which version is canonical, which is the one that gets reviewed, what the home release adds, and who has the contractual right to make each.
- **Engine.** Version as a first-class object with parent, runtime delta, content-ledger delta, its own classifications and its own audience reception. A director's cut must be able to be better-received than the theatrical one and still make less money.
- **Takes.** Each version 1-6 weeks of conform, mix and QC
- **Television.** Television versions are produced under time pressure per episode and multiply faster; a 10-episode series can carry six versions per episode before it has left the building.

### Language versioning

Dubbed and subtitled versions are produced — lip-sync dub, voice-over, or subtitle-only — with local voice casting, a translated and adapted script, and territory-specific censorship rewrites inside the dub.

- **Who.** Head of International Post; Dubbing Director; local casting for the dub; Subtitle Supervisor
- **Decides.** Which territories get a dub versus subtitles, who voices the lead abroad, and whether the dub softens content the local board would otherwise cut.
- **Engine.** Per-territory dub cost, lead time and preference weights, with the dubbed version carrying its own content ledger. A dub that launders profanity must be able to reach a lower band than the subtitled original in the same territory.
- **Takes.** Lip-sync dub 3-8 weeks per language; subtitles 1-2 weeks
- **Television.** A series must dub on a rolling per-episode basis to hold a day-and-date global release, which is a standing cost line rather than a one-off.

### Streaming maturity mapping and metadata delivery

The work is given the platform's maturity rating per territory (usually derived from the local statutory band or self-declared where none exists), plus genre tags, keyword tags, descriptors and artwork, and registered with EIDR and ISAN.

- **Who.** Platform Content Operations; the distributor's Delivery Manager; Metadata Editor
- **Decides.** Which rows the show appears in, which parental controls hide it, what the artwork promises, and how the recommender treats it.
- **Engine.** A taxonomy separate from the production's own genre record, with a mapping that can be wrong. Tags drive a recommender over the audience population. Change one tag and the discovery audience must change — that is the whole falsifiability test for metadata.
- **Takes.** Delivered 4-8 weeks before launch; tags revised continuously
- **Television.** Series carry season- and episode-level metadata, and an episode rated higher than its season can suppress the whole title under a parental control.

### Children's content designation

Content is designated for children, which triggers the US Children's Television Act commercial caps (10.5 minutes per hour at weekends, 12 on weekdays for under-12 audiences), E/I educational obligations, and COPPA made-for-kids status online.

- **Who.** Network Standards; the platform's compliance team; Head of Kids Programming
- **Decides.** How much advertising can run, whether host selling and programme-length commercials are permitted, and whether behavioural advertising and comments are switched off.
- **Engine.** A made-for-kids flag that genuinely reduces the advertising revenue per viewer and blocks ad classes, so that designating a show for children is a real trade. If the flag only changes a label, it fails.
- **Takes.** Designated at commission; reported quarterly to the FCC
- **Television.** This is broadcast and platform law with no theatrical equivalent. A G-rated film carries no advertising restrictions; a TV-Y programme carries several.

### Awards category placement

The picture is entered in categories its format qualifies for and its campaign can win: comedy or drama, series or limited series, movie or special, lead or supporting, feature or short.

- **Who.** Head of Awards; publicist; Academy or Television Academy rules staff
- **Decides.** Which trophy is chased, whether a performer is campaigned as lead or supporting, and whether a 40-minute show is campaigned as a half-hour.
- **Engine.** Real eligibility rules as code: Academy feature over 40 minutes including credits, short 40 or under; Emmy half-hour over 20 up to 40 minutes, hour over 40; limited series two or more episodes totalling at least 150 minutes; movie one part, 75 minutes minimum.
- **Takes.** Entries filed in a 2-4 week window; campaign 3-6 months
- **Television.** Emmy series eligibility needs six episodes premiered nationally by 31 May (three for documentary and hosted nonfiction), and a renewed limited series is disqualified from that category forever.

### Festival section and premiere status

The picture is submitted to a festival and placed in a section — competition, out of competition, a sidebar, midnight, or a market screening — with its premiere status (world, international, continental, national) as the currency.

- **Who.** Festival Strategist; Sales Agent; Programmer at the festival
- **Decides.** Where it premieres, what status it spends there, whether a sale happens, and whether it becomes an awards picture or a genre title.
- **Engine.** Premiere status as a consumable resource: a world premiere can be spent once, and spending it disqualifies the picture from sections that require one. Festivals are dated objects on the real calendar with real submission deadlines.
- **Takes.** Submission 2-5 months ahead; decision 4-10 weeks before the festival
- **Television.** Series premiere at festivals too, but the currency is press and talent visibility rather than a sale, since the show is usually already sold.

### Renewal, cancellation and format migration

At the end of a season the show is renewed (for how many episodes), cancelled, moved to another platform, shortened to a final limited run, or wrapped up as a TV movie.

- **Who.** Head of the network or streamer; Head of Current Programming; Studio Head (father)
- **Decides.** Whether the cast is released, whether options are exercised, and whether the show changes format on its way out.
- **Engine.** A renewal decision reading measured performance, cost per episode, licence fee, library value, quota value and the buyer's slate needs, with options exercised or lapsed on dated windows. A show can be cancelled while performing if it is too expensive.
- **Takes.** Decided in a 2-8 week window after the finale; options have hard dates
- **Television.** Film has nothing like it. This is the mechanism that turns an actor's series-regular option into either seven years of security or a sudden unemployment they did not choose.

### Retooling mid-run

An underperforming series changes: a new showrunner, a time jump, a relocation, characters written out and added, a tonal shift, or the procedural made serialized (or the reverse).

- **Who.** Showrunner (often a new one); Head of Current Programming; Studio executive
- **Decides.** What the show becomes, who is fired, and whether the original audience follows.
- **Engine.** A retool edits the story-generation record and carries an audience cost proportional to the distance moved, plus a chance of gain. The existing audience's loyalty is a real number that a retool spends. Small retools must be cheap and large ones must be dangerous.
- **Takes.** Decided between seasons or at a midseason break; 4-12 weeks to implement
- **Television.** Purely television. A feature's equivalent is reshoots, which happen before anyone has seen it and cannot lose an audience it has not yet got.

### Franchise and universe planning

A picture is planned as part of a connected set: sequels, prequels, spinoffs, crossovers and series extensions, with release corridors mapped years out and casts optioned for multiple pictures.

- **Who.** Studio Head (father); Head of Franchise Development; the creative committee
- **Decides.** Whether an actor signs a three-picture deal, which picture sets up which, and how much of one picture is spent setting up another.
- **Engine.** A franchise object holding slate order, dated release corridors, shared continuity state and multi-picture options on cast. A setup scene must cost the parent picture something measurable in its own reception while raising the successor's opening.
- **Takes.** Planned 3-8 years ahead; revised at every release
- **Television.** Television builds universes through spinoffs and crossovers that share a night and a lead-in, so the scheduling adjacency itself is part of the value.

### Special and event commissioning

A one-off is commissioned: holiday special, live musical, reunion, concert special, stand-up special, awards show, telethon, live episode of a scripted series, or an interactive branching special.

- **Who.** Head of Specials or Head of Events; Executive Producer; Line Producer
- **Decides.** Whether the event is live or recorded, how many cameras, whether there is an audience, and whether the scripted cast is paid on a different agreement.
- **Engine.** A one-off title with its own format, crew shape and risk. Live carries a real failure mode (transmission, performance, weather) that a recorded programme does not, and the failure has to be able to happen on air.
- **Takes.** Stand-up special 2-4 weeks; live musical 4-6 months
- **Television.** Specials are a television object with no film counterpart; the nearest film relative is event cinema, which distributes recorded stage or concert work through theatres.

### Short-form commissioning

Companion or standalone short-form is ordered: 2-20 minute episodes for a streaming or social platform, vertical video series, webisodes, or promotional narrative content.

- **Who.** Head of Digital; Social Producer; Platform partnerships executive
- **Decides.** Whether the player's actor works under a different agreement for less money and different residuals, and whether it counts as credit.
- **Engine.** Short-form as a distinct format with its own agreement, minimums, residual formula and awards eligibility (Emmy short form is 2-20 minutes average). Its credit value to a career must be genuinely lower, and measurable against the reputation axes.
- **Takes.** 1-5 shoot days for a season of short form
- **Television.** Short-form is the one format where television and film converge on the internet, and where the union floor is lowest, which is why it is where new talent is used and underpaid.

### Guild tier determination by runtime and budget

The programme's runtime band and budget place it in a specific minimum tier: SAG-AFTRA and WGA high-budget SVOD tiers key off 20-35, 36-65 and 66-95 minute bands crossed with per-episode budget thresholds.

- **Who.** Business Affairs; guild signatory administrator (WGA, SAG-AFTRA, DGA); Production Accountant
- **Decides.** What every writer, performer and director is owed as a floor, and therefore whether six more minutes of runtime is affordable.
- **Engine.** Minimums as a lookup on (format, runtime band, budget band, platform), wired to the payroll ledger. WGA treats a 36-65 minute high-budget SVOD programme as a 60-minute programme, 66-95 as 90, 96+ as 120. Add six minutes, the floor must jump.
- **Takes.** Determined at the deal; audited at delivery
- **Television.** Theatrical minimums key off total budget only. Television and streaming minimums key off runtime AND budget AND platform, so a show's length is directly a payroll decision.

### Content warning and statutory card placement

Required cards and warnings are placed: photosensitive epilepsy warnings, statutory anti-smoking disclaimers in India appearing throughout every scene with tobacco, drug and suicide helplines, and territory-specific classification cards before the feature.

- **Who.** Compliance Manager; Post Supervisor; the local distributor
- **Decides.** What is burned into the territory master, how long the front-of-picture runs, and whether the picture's opening is disfigured in some territories.
- **Engine.** Warning requirements derived from the content ledger × territory rules, producing per-territory master deltas with real cost and real runtime added. A cigarette in one scene must be able to add a permanent on-screen card in one territory and nothing in another.
- **Takes.** 1-3 days per territory master once the requirement is known
- **Television.** Broadcast additionally requires the classification bug on screen at the start and after each break in several territories, so the warning is recurring rather than front-loaded.

**Australia and New Zealand classification** — 16 options

`ACB: G — General` · `ACB: PG — Parental Guidance` · `ACB: M — Mature (advisory)` · `ACB: MA 15+ — Mature Accompanied, legally restricted` · `ACB: R 18+ — Restricted` · `ACB: X 18+ — sexually explicit, non-violent (legal only in the ACT and NT)` · `ACB: RC — Refused Classification (effectively banned)` · `ACB: CTC — Check the Classification (unclassified advertising material)` · `Australian television: P, C, G, PG, M, MA15+ with time zones (AV15+ abolished in 2015; C and P quotas removed in 2020-21)` · `NZ: G — Unrestricted` · `NZ: PG` · `NZ: M — suitable for mature audiences 16 and over (advisory)` · `NZ: R13, R15, R16, R18 — legally restricted` · `NZ: RP13, RP16, RP18 — restricted unless accompanied by a parent or guardian` · `NZ: R (restricted with a stated condition, e.g. to a specified audience)` · `NZ: Objectionable — banned`

**East and Southeast Asian classification** — 12 options

`Japan Eirin: G, PG12, R15+, R18+` · `South Korea KMRB: 전체관람가 (All), 12세이상관람가, 15세이상관람가, 청소년관람불가 (labelled 19 rather than 18 for titles rated from 1 May 2024), 제한상영가 (Restricted screening, licensed venues only)` · `Korean broadcasting: 전체 시청가, 7세, 12세, 15세, 19세 이상 시청가` · `Hong Kong OFNAA: I, IIA, IIB, III (III is legally restricted to 18+)` · `Taiwan: 普遍級 (0+), 保護級 (6+), 輔導十二歲級 (12+), 輔導十五歲級 (15+), 限制級 (18+)` · `China: no classification system at all — a Public Exhibition Licence (the Dragon Seal) from the China Film Administration, or refusal; online and television content filed with the NRTA` · `Singapore IMDA: G, PG, PG13, NC16, M18, R21 (video capped at M18; R21 is theatrical only), NAR (Not Allowed for All Ratings)` · `Malaysia LPF: U, P12, 13, 16, 18 (five-band system in force from 1 February 2023), plus banned` · `Indonesia LSF: SU (all), 13+, 17+, 21+` · `Philippines MTRCB: G, PG, R-13, R-16, R-18, X (not for public exhibition)` · `Thailand: ส (promoted), ท (general), น13+, น15+, น18+, ฉ20+ (identification checked), and banned` · `Vietnam: P (all), K (under 13 with a parent), T13, T16, T18, C (prohibited)`

**South Asian, Middle Eastern and African classification** — 15 options

`India CBFC: U — Unrestricted` · `India CBFC: U/A 7+` · `India CBFC: U/A 13+` · `India CBFC: U/A 16+` · `India CBFC: A — adults only` · `India CBFC: S — restricted to a specialised profession` · `India CBFC video prefixes: V/U, V/UA, V/A` · `Israel: All ages, 12+, 14+, 16+, 18+` · `UAE Media Council: G, PG, PG13, PG15, 18+, 21+` · `Saudi Arabia (GCAM / General Authority for Media Regulation): G, PG, PG12, PG15, R15, R18` · `Gulf regional practice: G, PG, PG-13, PG-15, 18+ used across GCC exhibition` · `Turkey: Genel İzleyici, 6+ (6A accompanied), 10+ (10A), 13+ (13A), 16+, 18+` · `South Africa FPB: A, PG, 7-9PG, 10-12PG, 13, 16, 18, X18, XX (XX is a refusal)` · `Nigeria NFVCB: G, PG, 12, 12A, 15, 18, RE (restricted exhibition)` · `Egypt: all ages, 12+, 16+, 18+, with censorship board cuts`

**Latin American classification** — 8 options

`Brazil ClassInd / DJCTQ: Livre, 10, 12, 14, 16, 18, with content descriptors and mandatory broadcast time bands rising with the band` · `Mexico RTC: AA (under 7), A (all), B (12+), B-15 (15+), C (18+), D (adults only, restricted venues)` · `Argentina INCAA: ATP (all), +13, +16, +18, +18 con reserva (restricted screening)` · `Chile (Consejo de Calificación Cinematográfica): TE (all), TE+7, 14, 18, plus '18 violencia excesiva' and '18 pornográfico'` · `Colombia: Todos los públicos, 7, 12, 15, 18` · `Peru: APT, 14, 18` · `Venezuela: A, B, C, D, E` · `Uruguay, Ecuador, Bolivia: commonly aligned to neighbouring bands, set by municipal or ministry authorities`

**Canadian classification (provincial)** — 9 options

`British Columbia / Saskatchewan (Consumer Protection BC): G, PG, 14A, 18A, R, E` · `Alberta: G, PG, 14A, 18A, R` · `Manitoba: G, PG, 14A, 18A, R, A` · `Maritime provinces (Nova Scotia, New Brunswick, PEI, Newfoundland): G, PG, 14A, 18A, R` · `Ontario: content information rather than classification since the Film Content Information Act, 2020 — exhibitors must post age recommendation and content elements` · `Quebec: Visa général (G), 13+, 16+, 18+, with an optional 'not suitable for young children' note` · `Canadian Home Video Rating System (CHVRS): G, PG, 14A, 18A, R, E` · `AGVOT television (English): C, C8, G, PG, 14+, 18+` · `Quebec French-language television: G, 8+, 13+, 16+, 18+`

**Rating outcomes other than a band** — 12 options

**Awards and eligibility formats** — 14 options
