**Version types** — 19 options

`Theatrical cut` · `International cut` · `Director's cut` · `Extended edition` · `Unrated cut` · `Television broadcast edit` · `Airline edit` · `Platform edit (streamer-specific)` · `Territory censorship cut` · `Dubbed version (per language)` · `Subtitled version (per language)` · `Clean-text master (no burned titles)` · `Open-caption version` · `Audio-described version` · `Descriptive-subtitle (SDH) version` · `Colour-corrected re-release / restoration` · `Anniversary re-release` · `Black-and-white version` · `Sing-along or interactive version`

### What gets got wrong

- 'Limited series' is a marketing word everywhere except the Emmys, where it is arithmetic: two or more episodes, at least 150 program minutes, a complete non-recurring story resolved in the season, and no ongoing storyline or main characters afterwards. Renewing a limited series retroactively destroys that eligibility. The engine must let a business decision — a renewal — kill an awards path the player was campaigning for.
- The old 'comedy is 30 minutes, drama is 60' Emmy rule is not the rule. Current bands are short form 2-20 minutes average, half-hour over 20 up to 40, hour over 40. A 38-minute drama competes as a half-hour in craft categories, and category placement is reviewable by the Academy's Industry Panel — so a format decision can be overruled by a body outside the production.
- An anthology that resets per episode and one that resets per season are different products and different Emmy categories. The episode-anthology enters as an anthology series; the season-anthology enters as a limited series. Their casting economics are opposite: one hires new leads every episode, the other every year.
- The MPA rating is voluntary. NC-17 is not illegal, it is unsellable — major chains decline to book it and many outlets refuse the advertising. Model the commercial refusal chain, never a legal block, or the whole American rating system becomes a censor it is not.
- There is no 'one f-word rule' in writing. CARA's practice treats a single non-sexual use of the harsher sexually-derived word as potentially PG-13 and more than one, or any sexual use, as initially R, appealable. Hardcoding 'count == 1' is exactly the fake-depth failure: the engine needs context tags, not a counter.
- BBFC classification is statutory for video under the Video Recordings Act 1984 and merely advisory in cinemas, where local authorities license screenings and may raise, lower or ban a certificate in their own area. The same picture can be legally 18 in one English borough and unshowable in the next.
- Age bands are not a monotonic scale that maps across territories. A US R is a UK 15 far more often than a UK 18; Italy admits accompanied 12s to a 14 and accompanied 16s to an 18; New Zealand's RP bands are legally distinct from its R bands. Any single 'severity score' with per-territory thresholds will be wrong in a way players notice.
- China has no classification system. A picture receives a Public Exhibition Licence (the Dragon Seal) or it does not, and the review chain runs roughly 30 working days at provincial level, 3-5 for the licence and 10-15 for technical review. There is no 'cut to a lower band' move — only a cut to approval — and it is a schedule item, not a marketing one.
- The FCC does not cap commercial minutes in adult programming. The 10.5-minutes-per-hour weekend and 12-minutes-per-hour weekday caps apply only to programming aimed at children 12 and under. The 22-minute and 42-minute conventions are commercial practice, they have drifted downward for thirty years, and they are properties of the buyer, not of television.
- UK 'series' means US 'season'. A UK sitcom series is six episodes, a UK drama series is three to eight, and a UK 'series two' is a second season. Shipping a UK broadcaster that orders 22 episodes is the single most obvious tell that the territory model is American with accents.
- A telenovela is not a soap. It has a planned chapter count, it ends, and it releases its cast; a soap is open-ended and does not. They share a strip-shooting production line and nothing else about their economics, their casting or their actors' career arcs.
- Multi-camera is not 'a sitcom with more cameras'. It is a five-day cycle — table read, two days of run-throughs with network and studio notes, camera blocking, then a tape night in front of a live audience — and the script changes overnight in response to how the audience laughed. If the engine schedules it as a shoot day with a different label, the entire form is missing.
- Anime is not 'an animated series set in Japan'. It is a production committee (a cap table where a publisher, a broadcaster, a music label and a toy company each take a rights window), ordered in cours of 12-13 weekly episodes against the real broadcast quarters. The seller is a committee, not a studio, and the revenue splits by window rather than down one waterfall.
- Documentary 'mode' and documentary subject are two different fields. A picture is observational AND true crime, or expository AND natural history. Collapsing them into one genre list makes the shooting ratio — which is where documentary money actually goes, at 12:1 for expository and 200:1 for observational — impossible to compute.
- 'Not Rated' and 'Unrated' are different things and players who know the industry will notice. NR was never submitted; Unrated is a version released without submitting that particular cut, usually on home video after a theatrical R. They have different legal and retail consequences.
- Rating appeals can be lost. A CARA appeal requires a two-thirds majority to find the rating clearly erroneous, costs weeks and political capital, and upholds often. An appeal mechanic that usually wins is a slot machine with an essay attached.
- In the Netherlands, Germany, Brazil and Australia the classification band IS the broadcast time slot. Rating a show 16 in Germany removes every hour before 22:00; Kijkwijzer 12 and above removes everything before 20:00. Rating and scheduling are one decision in those territories and two in the United States — the engine cannot use one model for both.
- The US television rating is applied by the network's own Standards and Practices department, not by an external board. That inverts the whole mechanic relative to film: it is a negotiation with a named executive whose goodwill is finite and spendable, and the Monitoring Board only appears on complaint.
- From 1 May 2024 Korea's youth-restricted band is labelled 19 rather than 18 on newly rated titles. Artwork and metadata carrying the old numeral get rejected at delivery. Classification labels are versioned by date, and a territory's bands must be a function of the in-game year, not a constant.
- Australia abolished AV15+ in 2015 and removed the C and P children's quota obligations in 2020-21. India subdivided U/A into 7+, 13+ and 16+ under the 2023 Act and the 2024 Rules. Italy moved to self-classification in 2021. A rating table hardcoded from memory is a 1990s table, and it will be wrong in six territories at once.
- India requires statutory anti-smoking cards and a scrolling disclaimer throughout every scene depicting tobacco. A single prop cigarette permanently disfigures the Indian master and adds runtime. Rating consequences are not always a band — sometimes they are burned-in artwork.
- Runtime is a payroll decision. WGA pays a 36-65 minute high-budget SVOD programme as a 60-minute programme, 66-95 as a 90, and 96 or more as a 120; SAG-AFTRA's high-budget SVOD thresholds cross runtime bands with per-episode budget. Six minutes added in the edit can raise every minimum on the show.
- Format rights in unscripted are protected commercially, not by copyright in the idea. What sells is the produced bible plus a ratings track record; FRAPA registration is evidence in a dispute, not a right. A game where 'you own the format' behaves like a patent is modelling law that does not exist.
- Premiere status is a consumable. A world premiere can be spent once, and spending it disqualifies the picture from sections at every other festival that requires one. Festivals differ on what counts as a public screening. Treating premiere status as a flag rather than a resource loses the entire festival strategy game.
- Genre affects exportability asymmetrically and severely. Action and horror travel; comedy does not, and dialogue comedy travels worst of all. A presale model with one global genre multiplier will price a comedy exactly as wrong as it prices a creature feature.
- A procedural and a serialized drama at the same budget have different assets. The procedural is worth real money in syndication and repeats because episodes are order-independent; the serial is worth more per binge and almost nothing in strip. If library value does not actually differ, the procedural/serial axis is a word with nothing reading it.
- 'Miniseries', 'limited series' and 'event series' are the same object renamed three times across four decades, and buyers still use all three. The save should hold one format with an era-appropriate display name rather than three formats that behave identically — three names for one thing is the same failure as one scalar wearing seven names.

---

## 4. Characters, roles and arcs

### Character bone-structure dossier

Builds each character on Egri's three axes — physiological, sociological, psychological — so that behaviour is derivable rather than asserted. Ten to forty pages for a principal set.

- **Who.** Screenwriter (feature) / Series creator (TV)
- **Decides.** Age, health, build, class of origin, schooling, trade, religion, politics, family position, temperament, ambition, frustrations, IQ, sexual outlook. The player sets each field or delegates to a staff writer.
- **Engine.** Every field must be read by something. Sociology fields feed the breakdown's stated identity and therefore the casting pool; psychology fields feed a decision function that returns what the character does under pressure, so two dossiers differing in one field produce different scene outcomes.
- **Takes.** 2-8 hours per principal; 3-10 days for a full principal set
- **Television.** TV dossiers must also answer 'what is this person's job every week' — a character with no renewable engine dies by episode six. Film needs the character to survive 110 pages once.

### Orchestration of the cast

Arranges the cast so no two characters would make the same choice in the same room. Egri's term. Unorchestrated casts produce scenes where anyone could say anyone's line.

- **Who.** Screenwriter / Showrunner
- **Decides.** Which axes are placed in opposition, how far apart, and whether the opposition is unbreakable (unity of opposites) or dissoluble — i.e. can these two walk away from each other?
- **Engine.** A pairwise distance matrix over dossier axes plus a per-pair 'can they leave' flag. Scene quality draws on the distance of the characters present; a scene of two characters at distance zero scores as flat and the script's flatness count rises.
- **Takes.** 1-3 days, redone every draft
- **Television.** TV must orchestrate for permanence: the pair must be unable to leave for seven years. Film can let the unity of opposites resolve at the climax.

### Dramatic function assignment

Assigns each character a function in the story's machine and checks that no required function is vacant and none is doubled by accident.

- **Who.** Screenwriter / Story editor
- **Decides.** Which of the function sets is in force (Vogler, Propp, Greimas, Dramatica), who occupies each slot, and which characters carry two functions at once — the mentor who is also the threshold guardian.
- **Engine.** A function slot table with occupancy rules per set — Dramatica forbids two Protagonists; Propp permits one person in several spheres. Vacancy and collision are computed, not declared, and a vacant antagonist slot lowers the script's conflict score.
- **Takes.** 2-6 hours at outline; rechecked each draft
- **Television.** TV reassigns functions weekly: this week's antagonist is next week's ally. The engine needs a per-episode function overlay on top of the series-level one.

### Lie, Ghost, Want and Need specification

Names the false belief the character holds, the backstory wound that installed it, the conscious goal that expresses it, and the truth that would dissolve it. Weiland's four-part spine.

- **Who.** Screenwriter
- **Decides.** The exact wording of the Lie, the event that is the Ghost, the object of the Want, and the Need. Also whether the Need is ever articulated aloud or only enacted.
- **Engine.** Each is a typed record, not prose. The Lie is a predicate the character applies to decisions; the Want is a goal object the character pursues in scene selection; the Need is a predicate that, once true, changes which choice the character takes. Flip the Lie and the third-act choice must invert.
- **Takes.** 1-4 hours per character
- **Television.** A series regular's Lie must be strong enough to survive a hundred episodes without resolving; resolving it early is the standard cause of a character going soft in season four.

### Arc shape selection

Chooses the shape of the character's change: positive, flat, or one of the three negatives, and its variant.

- **Who.** Screenwriter / Showrunner
- **Decides.** Which shape each character carries, and whether the ensemble's shapes are complementary (one flat arc changing the world around several change arcs) or redundant.
- **Engine.** Shape is a state machine with named states and legal transitions, not a label. A positive arc must pass through belief-in-Lie, first doubt, cost, relapse, and truth; the engine refuses to mark the arc complete if a state was skipped, and the script's coherence score drops.
- **Takes.** 1-2 hours per character, revisited at every draft
- **Television.** TV runs a flat arc at series level for the star and change arcs at season level around them. Film almost never sustains more than three full change arcs.

### Arc beat placement against structure

Pins each arc state to a page or a scene so the internal change lands on the external turns — the low point of the arc on the all-is-lost, the truth on the climactic choice.

- **Who.** Screenwriter
- **Decides.** Which page each arc beat sits on, and which arc beats share a scene with which plot beats. Whether the arc leads the plot or trails it.
- **Engine.** Two ordered sequences — plot beats and arc beats — with a distance metric between them. Drift beyond a threshold produces the diagnosis 'plot and character are running in different films', which the notes system then raises.
- **Takes.** 1-3 days on a card pass
- **Television.** TV pins arc beats to episode numbers across a season, and must survive an order cut from thirteen to ten mid-season, which shifts every beat.

### Idiolect and voice differentiation pass

A dedicated draft in which each character's dialogue is checked for a distinct register: sentence length, vocabulary level, whether they ask or assert, contractions, profanity, hesitation.

- **Who.** Screenwriter
- **Decides.** Per-character values on each speech axis, and which characters are deliberately allowed to sound alike (siblings, partners of thirty years).
- **Engine.** Measurable per-character statistics computed off the actual dialogue lines: mean words per speech, type-token ratio, question ratio, interruption count. The name-covered test — strip names and see if a reader can attribute — is computable and returns a number.
- **Takes.** 2-5 days over a feature draft
- **Television.** On a series, voice is enforced across episodes written by eight different writers; the script coordinator keeps a voice sheet and the showrunner does a dialogue polish on every episode for exactly this.

### Relationship web design

Lays out every pairing in the cast and gives each one a state: what they are to each other, what each wants from the other, and what is unsaid.

- **Who.** Showrunner / Screenwriter
- **Decides.** Which pairs exist at all, each pair's type, its temperature, its history, its secret, and which pairs are deliberately never put in a room together.
- **Engine.** A directed graph with an edge record per ordered pair — asymmetric, because what A wants from B is rarely what B wants from A. Scene assignment reads the graph; an unused edge is a story asset the engine can surface as 'these two have never met'.
- **Takes.** 1-2 days; on a series, maintained forever
- **Television.** TV depends on the unused-edge list: the pairing you have saved for season three is inventory. Film generally spends every edge.

### Ensemble architecture selection

Chooses the shape of the cast as a whole — hub and spoke, true ensemble, workplace, family, mosaic — before any character is written.

- **Who.** Series creator / Showrunner
- **Decides.** The architecture, the number of series regulars, how many stories run per episode, and whether the lead can be absent from an episode without the show collapsing.
- **Engine.** Architecture sets hard constraints the rest of the sim obeys: story slots per episode, the minimum and maximum regulars, how many episodes a regular may sit out, and the cost model, since a true ensemble pays more weekly salaries than a hub.
- **Takes.** Decided once, at pitch; hours to days
- **Television.** This is the most consequential TV-only decision in the domain. Film has a cast; TV has an architecture, and the architecture determines the weekly budget for eight years.

### Character breakdown descriptor authoring

Writes the stated identity of the character as it will go out to agents: age range, gender, ethnicity, body, disability, accent, and the one line of who they are.

- **Who.** Screenwriter, revised by the Casting director
- **Decides.** Every descriptor and, crucially, which are firm and which are open. 'Open ethnicity' widens the pool; a stated accent narrows it to performers who hold it or can acquire it.
- **Engine.** Each descriptor is a filter with a hardness value against the performer population. Loosening one descriptor must measurably change the size and quality distribution of the submission pool; if it does not, the descriptors are decoration.
- **Takes.** 10-30 minutes per role, revised at breakdown
- **Television.** TV breakdowns carry the recurring potential line ('recurring, possible series regular') which changes who will read for it, because a working actor weighs the option against other pilots.

### Series bible character section

Writes the document that tells a network and a future writing staff who these people are, what they do weekly, and where they are going over several seasons.

- **Who.** Series creator
- **Decides.** Which characters are pitched as regulars, which as recurring, the multi-season destination for each, and how much of that destination is disclosed to the buyer.
- **Engine.** The bible is a contract with the simulation: the stated destinations become targets the arc tracker measures drift against, and a network note that contradicts the bible is a state change with a cost, not a line of prose.
- **Takes.** 1-4 weeks; 15-60 pages
- **Television.** Film has no equivalent. The closest analogue is a franchise story document, and it binds nobody.

### Season arc board

The room breaks the season on a corkboard: a horizontal lane per character, a vertical column per episode, and a card wherever that character has a beat.

- **Who.** Showrunner with the writing staff (Co-EP, Supervising Producer, Executive Story Editor, Story Editor, Staff Writer)
- **Decides.** Which character's arc drives which episode, where each arc's turns sit, which episodes a regular is light in, and which arcs are deferred to next season.
- **Engine.** A characters-by-episodes matrix of beat cards, each typed to an arc state. Lanes with long empty stretches are computable and flag a regular the show has stopped using — which then collides with their episode guarantee and costs money.
- **Takes.** 2-6 weeks at the top of a season
- **Television.** Film's equivalent is a single card wall for one story. The board is the reason TV writers are producers: the board is a production schedule and a payroll document at the same time.

### Episode assignment and story-strand allocation

Assigns each episode to a writer and decides which character carries the A story, which the B, and which the runner.

- **Who.** Showrunner
- **Decides.** Writer per episode, A/B/C strand ownership, and which regulars are in the episode at all — which directly sets who gets paid that week.
- **Engine.** An assignment with per-episode character presence flags feeding both the arc tracker and the payroll. Cutting a regular from an episode saves an episodic fee only if their guarantee is not already met; the engine must compute that.
- **Takes.** One room day per season, adjusted continuously
- **Television.** Wholly TV. The film analogue does not exist.

### Character name and likeness clearance

Runs every character name, occupation and location against real people to avoid a defamation or false-light claim, and issues a clearance report marking names as cleared, cleared-with-change, or refused.

- **Who.** Script clearance researcher; Production counsel
- **Decides.** Whether to keep a refused name, change it, or accept the risk with an indemnity. Whether to clear an obviously real-world-adjacent character at all.
- **Engine.** A name register with per-name risk flags, a change cost that propagates through every draft and every already-shot scene, and an E&O premium that moves with the number of uncleared names. A refused name kept anyway raises the litigation hazard rate.
- **Takes.** 3-10 business days for a feature report
- **Television.** TV clears per episode, forever, on a one-week turnaround, and a recurring character's name is cleared once and inherited.

### Composite and fictionalisation decisions

On a true story, decides which real people are merged into one character, which are invented, and which are named. Each choice changes both the drama and the legal exposure.

- **Who.** Screenwriter with Production counsel
- **Decides.** The composite map: which real persons map to which characters, who gets a life rights deal, who is depicted under a changed name, and who is cut.
- **Engine.** A real-person-to-character mapping with a per-person consent state (life rights signed, refused, deceased, public figure) and a derived legal risk that gates whether the picture can be insured at all.
- **Takes.** Weeks of writing; 2-6 weeks of legal review
- **Television.** Anthology and limited series carry this at series level and per episode; a returning true-crime format re-runs the whole process every season.

### Mid-run character introduction

Adds a new character to a running series, usually to fix a hole the room has diagnosed — no one to argue with the lead, or an ensemble that has paired off.

- **Who.** Showrunner with the Network's current programming executive
- **Decides.** The function the new character fills, whether they enter as recurring or as a regular, and whether the audience is told they are permanent.
- **Engine.** Requires the function-vacancy computation to be live, a casting cycle mid-season, a cost delta on the pattern budget, and an audience-acceptance roll whose odds depend on how long the show has run and how loved the existing cast is.
- **Takes.** 4-8 weeks from decision to on screen
- **Television.** Film's only analogue is a reshoot-added character, which is rare and expensive. This is routine TV.

### Character write-out or death

Removes a character, by death, departure, or fade. Often driven by contract, behaviour or budget rather than story.

- **Who.** Showrunner; approved by the Studio and Network
- **Decides.** Whether the exit is fatal or reversible, how many episodes it takes, whether the actor is told in advance, and whether the death is used as a season-finale asset.
- **Engine.** A character state transition with consequences that must actually propagate: the relationship graph loses edges, dependent arcs are orphaned, the salary leaves the pattern budget, and the audience model takes a hit weighted by the character's measured attachment.
- **Takes.** Decided weeks to months ahead; executed over 1-3 episodes
- **Television.** Film kills characters for the story. TV kills them for the payroll, the schedule, or the headline, and a reversible death is a real and much-used option.

### Recasting

Replaces the performer while keeping the character. Done for availability, death, behaviour or money.

- **Who.** Showrunner and Casting director; Business affairs executes
- **Decides.** Whether to recast, acknowledge, or write out; whether the recast is announced; whether the new performer matches the old or is allowed to reinterpret.
- **Engine.** Character and performer must be separate objects with a history of bindings — a character with a cast list, not a character who is an actor. Audience acceptance of a recast is computed from episodes elapsed, attachment, and whether the change was acknowledged on screen.
- **Takes.** 2-6 weeks
- **Television.** Film recasts before or during shooting and reshoots. TV recasts between seasons and hopes; a mid-season recast is nearly always acknowledged.

### Spinoff and backdoor pilot extraction

Takes a character out of a running show and builds a series around them, sometimes by shooting the pilot as an episode of the parent show.

- **Who.** Showrunner and the Network's development executive
- **Decides.** Which character, whether they leave the parent or split time, who owns them between two production entities, and whether the parent show can survive the loss.
- **Engine.** Character ownership must be a rights record with a producing entity, a profit definition and a consent chain. The parent show's audience model must respond to the loss, and the performer's deal must be renegotiated across two shows.
- **Takes.** A season from decision to launch
- **Television.** Almost entirely TV. Film's version is the sequel-about-the-sidekick, which is a rights question rather than a scheduling one.

### Crossover negotiation

Moves a character from one series into another, which requires two studios, two networks, two schedules and two performer deals to align.

- **Who.** Business affairs executives of both shows; Showrunners agree the story
- **Decides.** Which characters cross, in which direction, how many episodes, who pays the performer, and how the credit reads.
- **Engine.** Needs inter-series character lending as a contract object: fee, credit, episode count, and a schedule constraint that actually blocks the lending show's shoot days. Without the schedule collision it is a cosmetic feature.
- **Takes.** 4-12 weeks of negotiation
- **Television.** TV-native. Film's shared-universe cameo is the same mechanism with longer lead times and bigger fees.

### Script breakdown by character

Goes through the script and counts, per character, the scenes they appear in, the lines they speak, the pages they carry, and the days those scenes fall on.

- **Who.** 1st Assistant Director; checked by the Script supervisor
- **Decides.** Nothing by itself — but this is the measurement that converts a written character into a role size and therefore into a contract tier and a cost.
- **Engine.** Per-character scene list, line count, word count and eighths of a page, derived from the script object rather than entered by hand. Change a scene and every downstream number must move, because role size, contract tier and payroll all read these.
- **Takes.** 1-2 days for a feature; 2-4 hours for an episode
- **Television.** On a series this is redone every episode, and a recurring character's cumulative line count across a season is what an agent uses to argue for a regular's deal.

### Day Out of Days construction

Builds the grid showing, for every cast member, which days they start, work, hold, travel and finish. The single document that turns a cast into a cost.

- **Who.** 1st Assistant Director / Unit Production Manager
- **Decides.** How scenes are grouped so a performer's work days cluster, whether to pay hold days or let someone go and bring them back, and who is on a drop-pickup.
- **Engine.** A cast-by-shoot-day matrix with SWFHT codes, driven by the schedule and driving the payroll. The player moving one scene must change someone's day count and therefore their fee — and a day performer crossing into a third day must reclassify to a 3-day contract.
- **Takes.** Half a day to build; updated every day of the shoot
- **Television.** TV builds a DOOD per episode inside a cross-boarded block, so a guest star's days may span two episodes and two sets of paperwork.

### Role size classification

Assigns every speaking part a size — lead through under-five — which sets the audition process, the fee band, the billing and the residual exposure.

- **Who.** Casting director with the Line producer
- **Decides.** Where each role sits on the size ladder, and which roles are deliberately over-classified to attract a better performer than the part warrants.
- **Engine.** Classification must be derived from the measured script numbers plus a manual override, with the override having a cost. Under-five has a hard rule — five lines or fewer and under fifty words — that the engine can check against the actual dialogue.
- **Takes.** Minutes per role, at breakdown time
- **Television.** TV's ladder has rungs film does not: co-star, guest star, special guest star, recurring, series regular. Film's ladder stops at day player and supporting.

### Cast list and character numbering

Fixes the official cast list with a number per character, used on every call sheet, production report and continuity log for the run of the show.

- **Who.** Script supervisor / Production coordinator
- **Decides.** The order of the list, which effectively publishes the production's own view of billing before billing is negotiated.
- **Engine.** A canonical character id used by every other system — schedule, payroll, continuity, credits. If the id namespace is not read by the payroll and the credit crawl, it is a declared word with nothing reading it.
- **Takes.** With the first breakdown; maintained throughout
- **Television.** TV's list is stable across a season and new numbers are appended, so number order becomes a fossil record of who arrived when.

### Breakdown writing and release

Writes the public breakdown for each role and releases it, normally through Breakdown Services, to agents and managers.

- **Who.** Casting director / Casting associate
- **Decides.** Which roles go out wide, which go out to a restricted list, which are offer-only and never released, and what is disclosed about the arc.
- **Engine.** Release scope must change the submission pool measurably: a wide release yields hundreds of submissions of low mean quality, a restricted list yields a dozen of high quality, and offer-only yields none but costs a fee premium.
- **Takes.** Hours to write; submissions land within 24-72 hours
- **Television.** Episodic guest roles go out 3-10 days before they shoot, which is why episodic casting is a throughput problem and film casting is a search problem.

### Offer-only versus audition decision

Decides whether a role is auditioned at all. Established performers will not read; the part goes to them as an offer or not at all.

- **Who.** Casting director with the Showrunner or Director
- **Decides.** For each role: audition, taped audition, meeting, or straight offer. And if offer, the order of the offer list, since a passed offer burns time and the schedule may not survive three passes.
- **Engine.** An offer list with per-performer response latency and pass probability, against a hard casting deadline tied to the shoot date. Each pass consumes days; running out of days forces a downgrade to a lesser performer, which is a real cost.
- **Takes.** Days to weeks per role
- **Television.** TV guest roles can be offer-only for name actors while the co-stars in the same episode are taped the day before. Both processes run in parallel on the same episode.

### Contract tier selection

Picks which SAG-AFTRA employment category the performer is engaged under, which sets the minimum, the overtime rules, the rest period and the residual base.

- **Who.** Business affairs executive / Line producer
- **Decides.** Day performer, 3-day, weekly, multiple-picture weekly, Schedule F, major role, modified deal or guest performer, or a series contract at a given episode guarantee.
- **Engine.** A rate table keyed by category, budget tier, programme length and effective date. Under the 2026-30 agreement: day performer $1,283; weekly $4,456; 3-day $3,252 (half or one hour); major role half-hour $7,057, one-hour $11,290, each 07/01/26-06/30/27.
- **Takes.** Minutes, at deal memo time
- **Television.** Television has categories theatrical lacks — 3-day, major role, modified deal and modified guest performer, series contracts by episode guarantee. Theatrical has multiple-picture weekly, which television does not.

### Schedule F election

Elects to engage a performer above the Schedule F break, which exempts the deal from many minimum working conditions in exchange for a much higher guaranteed salary.

- **Who.** Business affairs executive; agreed with the Agent
- **Decides.** Whether to go Schedule F at all, since it buys away overtime and certain penalties but sets a high floor and signals the performer's standing.
- **Engine.** A threshold check with real numbers: the 2026 MOA raised the theatrical break from $65,000 to $80,000, half-hour TV and new media from $32,000 to $37,500, and one hour and over from $32,000 to $45,000. Above it, the overtime engine is switched off for that performer.
- **Takes.** Part of the same deal; minutes once the number is agreed
- **Television.** Television's break is per episode and splits by programme length; theatrical's is per picture. A performer can be Schedule F on a film and daily scale on a TV guest spot in the same month.

### Quote check and quote protection

Establishes what the performer was last paid for comparable work. The quote is the floor for the next negotiation, which is why agents protect it and refuse work that would lower it.

- **Who.** Talent agent; verified by Business affairs
- **Decides.** Whether to take a job below quote, and if so whether to protect the quote by taking the shortfall as deferred money, points, a perk, or a favoured-nations clause.
- **Engine.** A per-performer quote history by medium and role size, queryable by other productions. Taking a low-paid job must actually lower the number the next production offers, or quote protection is meaningless.
- **Takes.** Hours to two days
- **Television.** TV quotes are per episode and per role size; a film quote does not transfer to an episodic fee, and agents fight hard to keep the two ledgers separate.

### Episode guarantee negotiation

Sets how many episodes of each ordered season the performer is paid for regardless of whether they appear.

- **Who.** Talent agent / Attorney with the Business affairs executive
- **Decides.** The ratio — all-produced, 13 of 13, 10 of 13, 7 of 13, 6 of 13 — and what happens on a short order, a split season, or a back nine.
- **Engine.** A guarantee record that the payroll honours: episodes appeared versus episodes paid, with the shortfall paid out at season end. The series-contract weekly minimum itself moves with the guarantee — half-hour, 13 or more guaranteed is $4,456/week; guaranteed 6 is $5,947.
- **Takes.** Days to weeks
- **Television.** Entirely TV. The lower the guarantee, the higher the per-episode minimum, because the union prices insecurity — a real inversion the engine must reproduce.

### Billing negotiation

Negotiates where the performer's name appears, in what size, in what order, and with what word attached.

- **Who.** Talent agent with Business affairs; often the last point closed
- **Decides.** Main title or end credits, position in the crawl, card sharing, the 'and' position, 'with', 'special guest star', 'introducing', paid ad obligations and size percentage relative to the title.
- **Engine.** A credit object with position, card, wording and size, rendered into an actual credit sequence and an actual one-sheet. Two performers cannot both hold last position; the engine must detect the collision and force a trade of money for placement.
- **Takes.** Days; frequently the final open item
- **Television.** TV billing is fought once and inherited for the show's life, and the guest-star ladder (co-star, guest star, special guest star) is a TV-only currency. Film billing is renegotiated per picture.

### Option and exclusivity terms

Sets how long the production may hold the performer, in what seasons, and what else they may do in that time.

- **Who.** Business affairs executive
- **Decides.** Number of option seasons, exercise deadlines, exclusivity scope (all media, first position, second position on another series), and permitted outside work such as features in hiatus.
- **Engine.** Options are dated obligations that expire and must be exercised or lost, and exclusivity must actually block the performer from accepting other offers the engine generates. California Labor Code 2855 caps enforceable personal service at seven years from commencement.
- **Takes.** Days to weeks, inside the same deal
- **Television.** Six-season options are standard in TV and unknown in film. Second-position deals — where a performer may take another series if this one is not picked up — are a TV-only instrument.

### Test option deal

Negotiates the entire series deal in full before the performer is allowed to read for the studio and network, so that if they win the part there is nothing left to argue.

- **Who.** Business affairs executive with the Agent, before the network test
- **Decides.** Episodic fee, all season bumps, guarantee, options, billing, perks, and exclusivity — all agreed on an actor who may not get the job.
- **Engine.** A pre-agreed contract attached to a probability. Multiple performers hold test deals for the same role simultaneously; only one converts. The negotiation's terms must persist and bind if the option is exercised.
- **Takes.** 24-72 hours, often overnight before the test
- **Television.** Wholly TV. There is no film equivalent; the closest is a pay-or-play offer, which is a commitment rather than a contingent one.

### Favoured nations equalisation

Applies a clause guaranteeing that no comparable performer gets better terms, which converts one concession into many.

- **Who.** Business affairs executive
- **Decides.** Which performers are in the favoured-nations group, on which terms (money, billing, trailer, travel), and whether the group is disclosed.
- **Engine.** A constraint propagating across contracts: improving one member's term must automatically improve every other member's, and the engine must price that before the player agrees. A blind favoured-nations grant is how budgets detonate.
- **Takes.** Applied across the cast in days
- **Television.** Series ensembles often go favoured nations at launch, which makes the season-three renegotiation collective rather than individual — several regulars bargaining as one bloc.

### Overscale, perks and backend

Negotiates everything above minimum: the fee, the trailer, the travel, the assistant, the hairdresser, the per diem, the credit, and the participation in profits.

- **Who.** Talent attorney / Agent with the Studio's Business affairs
- **Decides.** Cash versus backend split, whether the participation is on gross, adjusted gross or net, the first-dollar breakpoint if any, and which perks are contractual rather than customary.
- **Engine.** A definitions object attached to the picture's actual revenue waterfall, so a points deal pays out from a real accounting model. A 'net profit' point that never pays and a first-dollar gross point that pays from the first ticket must behave differently.
- **Takes.** Weeks for a lead; the long pole on any star deal
- **Television.** TV backend is modified adjusted gross receipts on the series, paid over years of licensing and syndication; streaming has largely replaced it with higher fixed fees and bonus structures tied to viewership tiers.

### Pay-or-play declaration

Commits the production to paying the performer whether or not the picture is made or the episode shoots, in exchange for locking them.

- **Who.** Studio production executive
- **Decides.** Whether to go pay-or-play at all and on what date, since the date is when the money becomes unavoidable and the greenlight becomes psychologically irreversible.
- **Engine.** A dated liability posted to the ledger on declaration, not on shooting. Abandoning the picture after the date must still pay, and the engine's abandonment decision must weigh that sunk obligation.
- **Takes.** A single decision; instantaneous and binding
- **Television.** Series regulars are pay-or-play per guaranteed episode as standard, so a network holding a show off the schedule still pays the cast.

### Rider attachment

Attaches the specific written consents a role requires: nudity and simulated sex, stunts, hazardous work, digital replica, and minors.

- **Who.** Business affairs executive; enforced by SAG-AFTRA
- **Decides.** What exactly is consented to, in what detail, and whether the performer refuses — which can force a rewrite, a double, or a recast.
- **Engine.** Each rider is a permission gate on a scene type. No nudity rider, no nudity scene; the scene must be rewritten, doubled or cut, with a cost. Nudity and simulated sex terms must be in writing with a reasonably specific description before the day.
- **Takes.** At contract; nudity terms in writing well ahead of the shoot day
- **Television.** A series regular signs once and the rider governs for seasons, which is why the specificity of the description matters far more in TV than on a single picture.

### Digital replica consent

Obtains clear and conspicuous consent, with a reasonably specific description of intended use, before creating or using a digital reproduction of the performer.

- **Who.** Business affairs executive with the Performer and their Attorney
- **Decides.** Whether to scan at all, employment-based or independently created replica, what uses are licensed, for how long, in which pictures, and what the compensation is.
- **Engine.** A consent record scoped to named uses and a duration, which the VFX and post systems must check before any shot using the replica can exist. A synthetic performer used in place of hiring requires a justification obligation, and the engine should hold that as a state.
- **Takes.** At contract; the scan itself is a half to full day
- **Television.** Replicas are more valuable in TV, where a recurring character can appear in episodes the performer never shot — which is exactly the practice the consent regime exists to govern.

### Minor engagement

Engages a performer under eighteen, which imposes work permits, a trust account, an on-set teacher, and hard daily limits on work, school and rest.

- **Who.** Unit Production Manager; Studio teacher on set; parent or guardian signs
- **Decides.** Whether to cast a minor at all, whether to cast an eighteen-year-old to play younger, whether to twin infants, and how to schedule around the hour limits.
- **Engine.** Age-banded hour rules that actually constrain the schedule, a studio teacher as a required crew position, a Coogan trust taking a statutory share of gross (15% in California) before the performer is paid, and a permit with an expiry.
- **Takes.** Permit 1-3 weeks; teacher on every school day of work
- **Television.** A child regular on a long-running series ages through the bands, so the same character's available hours expand year over year — a schedule variable that only exists in TV.

### Recurring escalation

Moves a performer up the ladder as the character sticks: day player to co-star to guest star to recurring with an episodic quote to series regular.

- **Who.** Talent agent with Business affairs
- **Decides.** When to push for the next rung, and whether to trade a higher fee for a guarantee, better billing, or a regular's deal next season.
- **Engine.** A performer-to-character engagement history where each new engagement reads the previous one. Refusing an escalation must have a real chance of the character being written out, and accepting must raise the pattern budget.
- **Takes.** Negotiated per episode or per arc; hours to days
- **Television.** This ladder is TV's core career mechanism and has no film analogue. Recurring performers increasingly do the work regulars used to do, at day-player money, which is a real and current pressure the sim should reproduce.

### Series regular renegotiation

Reopens the deal mid-option, usually around season three or after a hit, sometimes by the cast bargaining as a bloc and refusing to report.

- **Who.** Talent attorney and Agent against the Studio's Business affairs
- **Decides.** Whether to hold out, what to demand — episodic fee, backend, episode count, directing episodes, a producer credit — and how long to risk the production's schedule.
- **Engine.** A negotiation with leverage computed from the show's measured ratings, the character's measured attachment, the cost of writing them out, and how many episodes the studio has already committed. A holdout must actually halt production and cost money per day.
- **Takes.** 2-6 months, usually between seasons
- **Television.** Entirely TV. Film's equivalent is the sequel renegotiation, which is a fresh deal rather than a reopened one.

### Option lapse and cast release

Lets an option expire, releasing the performer and, usually, retiring the character.

- **Who.** Business affairs executive
- **Decides.** Which options to exercise before the deadline and which to let go, balancing money against the arcs already promised on the board.
- **Engine.** Dated deadlines the clock enforces without the player's attention. Missing an exercise date must release the performer into the market where a rival can sign them, and orphan every arc beat depending on them.
- **Takes.** Date-driven; the deadline is the process
- **Television.** TV's calendar of exercise dates clusters after a season ends and before the next is ordered; film has no recurring equivalent.

### Actor's script analysis

Breaks the script into units, names an objective for each, chooses an action to pursue it, and identifies the obstacle. The private work that produces the performance.

- **Who.** Performer
- **Decides.** The super-objective, the unit boundaries, the verb chosen for each beat, the substitutions and sense-memory used, and what the character is doing when not speaking.
- **Engine.** A per-scene record of objective, action verb, obstacle and tactic, feeding a performance quality computation. Changing the chosen verb must change the take's character — coverage that reads as pleading versus coverage that reads as testing.
- **Takes.** 4-20 hours for a feature lead; 1-3 hours for an episodic guest
- **Television.** An episodic guest analyses a role with no future and no context; a series regular analyses the same character across a hundred episodes and must track what they have already played.

### Technique and rehearsal method selection

Chooses how the role will be built — which school, how much research, whether the work is done in advance or found in the room.

- **Who.** Performer, sometimes with a private acting coach
- **Decides.** The method, the amount of preparation, whether to rehearse with the other performers or stay apart, and whether to stay in character between setups.
- **Engine.** Technique must be a trait with real trade-offs: preparation depth raises take quality but lowers adaptability to rewrites; staying in character raises consistency and lowers crew relations, which feeds the reputation and rehire models.
- **Takes.** Weeks of preparation; ongoing through the shoot
- **Television.** TV's speed punishes techniques that need long preparation. Pages change the morning of, so adaptability outweighs depth — an inversion of the film calculation.

### Backstory construction

Writes what the character did before page one — the biography the audience never sees but the performance rests on.

- **Who.** Performer, sometimes with the Screenwriter
- **Decides.** Everything the script leaves blank, and whether to check it against the writer's dossier or deliberately diverge.
- **Engine.** A performer-held dossier that can agree or conflict with the writer's dossier. A conflict is a live state that produces on-set disagreement, costs takes, and is resolved by the director — with a winner.
- **Takes.** Days to weeks
- **Television.** On a series the performer's backstory competes with a room that will later write canon contradicting it; long-running shows accumulate these collisions.

### Physical transformation

Changes the body for the role — weight gain or loss, muscle, a skill like boxing, riding or piano played on camera.

- **Who.** Performer with a trainer, nutritionist and the production's medical oversight
- **Decides.** How far to go, how long before the shoot to start, and whether the production pays for and schedules the preparation.
- **Engine.** A body state with rates of change bounded by physiology and health consequences, gated on a preparation window the schedule must actually give. Cutting the window must cap the achievable change and raise injury probability.
- **Takes.** 6 weeks to 8 months, depending on the change
- **Television.** TV cannot grant the window; a regular transforms across a hiatus or not at all, and any change must be sustainable for the season because episodes shoot out of order.

### Dialect and voice work

Acquires and maintains an accent or a voice quality, and polices it take by take.

- **Who.** Dialect coach with the Performer; coach stays on set
- **Decides.** Which target accent, how broad, whether it is consistent with the character's stated biography, and whether to drop it under emotional pressure as real speakers do.
- **Engine.** A per-performer dialect proficiency that rises with coached hours and decays without them, checked per take. Low proficiency must produce failed takes and, in post, ADR — a real downstream cost, not a note.
- **Takes.** 4-12 weeks of sessions before the shoot; on set every day
- **Television.** A series accent must hold for years across many directors. Dialect drift between seasons is a genuine continuity failure the script supervisor and coach track.

### Look development for the character

Builds the character's silhouette, hair and face, then tests it on camera under the picture's actual lighting.

- **Who.** Costume designer; Hair department head; Makeup department head; approved by the Director
- **Decides.** Silhouette, palette, period accuracy, how the look changes across the arc, and whether a change is legible on screen.
- **Engine.** A look state per character per story-day, bound to the arc states so the look can be made to move with the arc. Continuity then requires that any scene shot out of order matches the look for its story-day, which the schedule must respect.
- **Takes.** Fitting 2-4 hours; camera and makeup test a half day
- **Television.** A TV character's look is a brand and changes slowly and deliberately; a single film can transform a look three times in 110 pages.

### Table read

The cast reads the script aloud, which exposes length, pace, dead scenes and jokes that do not work, and gives the executives their first hearing.

- **Who.** Whole cast, Showrunner or Director, writers, studio and network executives
- **Decides.** What gets cut before shooting, which character is not landing, and occasionally that a role must be recast.
- **Engine.** A scored event reading the script's measurable properties and the cast's chemistry, producing per-scene verdicts and a runtime estimate. A bad read must have consequences: a rewrite, a cut, or a note from the network.
- **Takes.** 1-3 hours for an hour drama
- **Television.** Multi-camera comedy reads on Monday and rewrites through Thursday, so the read is the start of a weekly rewrite machine. Film reads once, weeks out, and often not at all.

### Rehearsal and blocking

Walks the scene on the set so the director can find the staging and the crew can see what they have to light and shoot.

- **Who.** Director with the Performers; 1st AD calls it; heads of department watch
- **Decides.** Where the character moves and why, which beats are played in which shot, and what the performer is permitted to change.
- **Engine.** Blocking must produce a shot list, which produces setups, which consume schedule minutes. A performer's proposed change that adds a setup must cost time the 1st AD may refuse to give.
- **Takes.** 15-45 minutes per scene on a single-camera set
- **Television.** Multi-camera comedy rehearses all week and blocks for four cameras at once. Single-camera TV rehearses for minutes. Film may rehearse for weeks before principal photography.

### Character continuity

Records, take by take, what the character wore, held, ate, said and did, so scenes shot weeks apart cut together.

- **Who.** Script supervisor
- **Decides.** Which take is the printed master for continuity, and what the character's established state is at every story-day boundary.
- **Engine.** A continuity log keyed to story-day and character state, with errors detectable at edit time. An unlogged mismatch must produce an actual editing problem — an unusable take — rather than a cosmetic note.
- **Takes.** Every take, all day, every day
- **Television.** A series script supervisor also guards multi-season canon, and on a long run the writers' assistant and script coordinator keep a show bible for exactly this.

### Doubling assignment

Assigns the bodies that stand in for the performer: the stand-in for lighting, the photo double for shots where the face is not seen, the body double, the stunt double, the digital double.

- **Who.** 2nd Assistant Director for stand-ins and photo doubles; Stunt coordinator for stunt doubles; VFX supervisor for digital doubles
- **Decides.** Which shots each double covers, whether the principal must be present, and whether the double's face may ever be visible — which changes the category and the pay.
- **Engine.** A shot-level requirement of who must be physically present, with per-double match attributes. A face-visible frame containing a photo double is a reclassification and a cost. Rates: stand-in and photo double $270/day, general background $231, special ability $241 (07/01/26-06/30/27).
- **Takes.** Assigned per day on the call sheet; stunt doubles cast weeks ahead
- **Television.** A series keeps the same stand-in for years — they learn the performer's rhythms and become de facto crew. Features hire fresh each picture.

### ADR and voice continuity

Re-records lines that were unusable, changed, or never shot, matching the performance and the dialect to picture months after the shoot.

- **Who.** ADR supervisor and Dialogue editor with the Performer
- **Decides.** Which lines to replace, whether to rewrite in the booth, and whether the character's voice can be altered in post to fix a performance problem.
- **Engine.** An ADR list generated from the sound report and the edit, with a per-line match quality that depends on how long since the shoot and the performer's dialect proficiency. Bad ADR must be audible and lower the picture's quality score.
- **Takes.** 2-6 hours per principal on a feature
- **Television.** TV loops fast and often, and a recurring character's voice may be looped by a different performer entirely for a line or two — which is a union and a consent question.

### Intimacy choreography and consent

Designs a scene of nudity or simulated sex as choreography, confirms the consent already given in the rider matches what will actually be shot, and controls the closed set.

- **Who.** Intimacy coordinator with the Director and the Performers
- **Decides.** Exactly what is shown and done, what modesty garments and barriers are used, who is on set, and what happens if a performer withdraws consent on the day.
- **Engine.** A scene-level plan that must match the rider's description; a mismatch must halt the scene. Withdrawal of consent on the day is a live state that forces a rewrite, a double, or a lost shot day, all of which cost.
- **Takes.** Rehearsal 1-4 hours; on set for the whole of the scene
- **Television.** A series regular's intimacy scenes recur with different directors each episode, so the coordinator's role is as much institutional memory as choreography.

### Network and studio character notes

Returns notes on each draft, disproportionately about character: likeability, clarity of want, whether the audience will root for them, whether the lead is passive.

- **Who.** Current programming executive (TV) / Creative executive (film)
- **Decides.** For the player on the writing side: which notes to take, which to appear to take, and which to refuse — and what refusal costs in the relationship.
- **Engine.** Notes must be typed objects that name the character and the axis, with an acceptance state. Taking a note must change the script object; refusing must change the executive relationship, which changes future greenlight probability.
- **Takes.** 24-72 hours after each draft; a notes call of 60-90 minutes
- **Television.** TV notes arrive every week for years and come from two buyers — studio and network — who often disagree, so the showrunner is arbitrating a standing conflict rather than answering a memo.

### Audience character testing

Puts a cut in front of a recruited audience and measures the characters: likeability, believability, who they would follow, who they wanted more of, who they wanted gone.

- **Who.** Research company (recruited screening); Studio research executive
- **Decides.** Whether to reshoot for a character, recut to change a character's arc, or change an ending on the strength of a number.
- **Engine.** A per-character audience score model driven by the character's actual properties — screen time, the arc shape, whether the Need was articulated, the performer's standing — not a random draw. Reshooting must move the score in a predictable direction.
- **Takes.** Screening plus 1-2 weeks to the report
- **Television.** TV tests pilots hard and then stops, substituting weekly ratings and social measurement. A pilot's character testing can force a recast before a single series episode is shot.

### Character likeability and Q Score tracking

Tracks the performer's and the character's familiarity and appeal in the public, separately from the show's ratings.

- **Who.** Marketing research executive; publicist reads it
- **Decides.** Whether to push a character forward in marketing, whether to campaign a performer for awards, and whether a character's decline is the performer's problem or the writing's.
- **Engine.** Two separate measures — performer standing and character attachment — that can diverge. Divergence must be usable: a beloved character on a disliked performer changes recasting and write-out calculations.
- **Takes.** Measured quarterly
- **Television.** TV's version runs continuously across seasons and is the number an agent brings to a season-three renegotiation. Film measures per picture.

### Awards category placement

Decides whether a performance is campaigned as lead or supporting, which is a strategic choice constrained by contract and by how much screen time the performance actually has.

- **Who.** Awards consultant with the Publicist and Business affairs
- **Decides.** Category for each campaigned performance, and whether to honour a contractual promise of a lead campaign that the cut no longer supports.
- **Engine.** Category placement must interact with measured screen time, billing position and the competitive field, producing a nomination probability. Category fraud must be possible and must carry a backlash risk.
- **Takes.** Decided at campaign launch; August to October for a film year
- **Television.** TV splits drama, comedy and limited series, and a performer can be campaigned as guest actor on the same show where they are contractually recurring — a category that does not exist in film.
