- Financing costs are a real and large budget line on an independent picture — interest, bank fees, legal, the bond fee, tax credit lending costs and the CAM's fee together commonly run to a meaningful fraction of the budget. A model in which money is free will never make the player feel the cost of a slow close.
- The bond negotiation is really a schedule negotiation. The guarantor's own line producer re-boards the script; if they think the director cannot make the schedule, the price of a bond is more days, and more days is more money. A player who submits an aggressive schedule to get a lower budget will be handed that budget back with days added.
- A budget in one currency financed in another can go over on the exchange rate alone, without a single bad decision being made. Real pictures have lost real money this way, and it is a clean way to make the choice of shooting country carry a risk as well as an incentive.
- The three playable people sit at different points in this system and must see different numbers. The actor sees account 1400 and their own quote, per diem, holding fee and backend; the mother sees the finance plan, the waterfall and the overage call; the father sees the charge-back as revenue on the lot's ledger and, as a writer, is the only person who can cut a picture to fit without wrecking it. The same overage should look like three different problems.

---

## 7. Casting, end to end

*film and television, from role record to call sheet row*

### Role Record & Breakdown Authoring

Every speaking part is lifted out of the script into a role record — age band, arc weight, scene and page count, skills, nudity and stunt flags — then written up as the breakdown paragraph agents read.

- **Who.** Screenwriter/showrunner (the player's father), with the director and casting director
- **Decides.** Which parts exist and how big; what the part demands (accent, riding, nudity, a real instrument); whether age, gender and ethnicity are written as required or open; the money band the role sits in.
- **Engine.** Role {scenes[], pages, lines, DOOD span, age band, identity requirement = required|open, skills[], nudity, stunt, minor, budget band, billing slot, arc weight}. Breakdown prose is generated from these fields and every later step reads them; nothing in casting reads free text.
- **Takes.** 1-3 days per script (est.), re-issued on every coloured revision
- **Television.** TV breaks the pilot plus the series bible: regulars are written as continuing with option ladders attached, guest roles are broken weekly off drafts that are not locked, sometimes before the scene exists.

### Casting Director Engagement & Fee Deal

A casting office is hired — flat fee on a feature, pilot fee plus per-episode fee on a series — with session space, readers, assistants and Breakdown fees budgeted separately.

- **Who.** Producer/UPM (the player's mother), with studio casting executive sign-off; the CD's agent
- **Decides.** Which office: the one with network relationships, the one known for discoveries, the one that travels to the incentive state, or the cheap local. Whether the same office also casts background.
- **Engine.** CD agent {taste vector, per-agency trust scores, discovery bias, studio/network credibility, roster memory of every actor ever seen, fee, capacity = sessions per week}. Fee hits the budget; capacity hard-caps how many actors can be seen per week, so hiring decides how wide a search is possible.
- **Takes.** Engaged 6-12 weeks before a feature shoot; before pilot greenlight in TV (est.)
- **Television.** Series carry the CD all season on an episodic fee, and most shows run three offices at once: pilot CD, episodic CD, and a local CD in the shooting city for day players and background.

### Cast Budget & Fringe Modelling

Cast money is laid out role by role — rate, guaranteed days, overtime curve, travel and per diem, fitting, rehearsal, looping days — with union fringes loaded on top of every dollar.

- **Who.** Line producer and production accountant
- **Decides.** How many days each role can afford, where scale-plus-ten stops and overscale begins, and which single role gets the money that buys a name.
- **Engine.** Per role: rate x guaranteed days + OT curve + travel/per-diem + fitting/rehearsal/ADR days, x fringe (P&H roughly 19-21% plus payroll taxes, est.). Sits in the cast account (1400 in the common feature template) and re-prices automatically whenever the DOOD changes.
- **Takes.** Continuous; locked at budget lock and re-run on every schedule version
- **Television.** TV budgets a repeating pattern: regulars amortised per episode, a fixed guest/day-player allowance per episode, and a season overage pool the showrunner burns on the weeks that need a name.

### Wish List, Comparables & Offer Order

Before anyone reads, the room builds a ranked name list per role and argues comparables: who did this exact thing, what it opened, what it cost, who is hot this quarter.

- **Who.** Director, producers, studio creative executive, casting director
- **Decides.** The order offers go out in, and whether a role is offer-only or auditioned at all.
- **Engine.** Name grid per role: rows = actors, columns = fit, cost, availability, sales value, studio appetite, director want, each a number. Sorted list is the offer chain; every pass pops the head and re-sorts, and the list decays as dates close.
- **Takes.** 1-2 weeks initially (est.); revisited after every pass
- **Television.** Pilots build the list against a fixed test date, so it fills with people who will actually test; streaming series skip tests for names entirely and go straight to offer.

### Cast-Contingent Financing & Sales Estimates

Foreign estimates are set territory by territory against a named cast, and a bank lends against them, so every cast change re-prices the picture before a frame is shot.

- **Who.** Sales agent, financier, completion guarantor, producer
- **Decides.** Whether to take the better actor and lose the German pre-sale, or pay the star who closes the financing gap.
- **Engine.** Territory x actor value table (genre-scaled ranges, est.). Gap = budget − (pre-sales + incentive + equity). Swapping one name recomputes estimates, gap, and whether the film is financed at all; below a threshold the project falls out of the schedule.
- **Takes.** Days to months; re-run on every cast change
- **Television.** No territory pre-sales. The equivalent gate is the network or platform casting executive's approval and an internal talent-value model that nobody outside the building sees.

### Quote Research & Precedent

Business affairs pulls what the actor was actually last paid, on what, in what role size, under what terms; the agent argues the number up and explains away anything cheap as a favour.

- **Who.** Business affairs executive; the actor's agent on the other side
- **Decides.** The opening number — and what your own deal will become as precedent for this actor's next three jobs.
- **Engine.** Actor.quoteHistory[] {title, role size, fee, year, terms, favour flag}. Quote = highest verified recent fee, decayed by time and tier movement. Accepting under quote writes a record other producers read later; quote protection side letters suppress that record.
- **Takes.** Hours to days
- **Television.** Television quotes are per-episode and travel across shows: a hit series regular's episodic number is the figure every subsequent negotiation starts from, whatever the new show is worth.

### Breakdown Release & Channel Choice

The breakdown goes to agents and managers through Breakdown Services, or is opened to actor self-submission via Actors Access or Casting Networks, or kept confidential under a fake title and a hand-picked list.

- **Who.** Casting director / casting associate
- **Decides.** Reach against control. Agents-only keeps the pile small and the tier high; open release floods it and surfaces unknowns; confidential protects the plot and slows everything.
- **Engine.** Channel {reach multiplier, tier distribution of responders, leak probability, hours to first submission, cost}. Drives the submission generator; leak probability rolls against the trade-press system and the project's NDA state.
- **Takes.** Out same day; submissions arrive in hours and peak at 24-72h (est.)
- **Television.** Series release weekly per episode with roles coded by function; pilots go out under a code title and the trades actively hunt breakdowns, so leak risk is highest exactly when secrecy matters most.

### Submission Intake & Sort

Hundreds to thousands of submissions per role arrive as headshot, résumé, reel and links; the office sorts them into preread, self-tape request, and no.

- **Who.** Casting assistant and associate
- **Decides.** As casting: how deep to read and whose calls to take. As the actor: nothing — only whether your materials survive a four-second look.
- **Engine.** For each represented actor, P(submitted) = f(fit to role fields, agent-CD relationship, tier match, availability, quote vs band, casting_profile_upkeep). Sort score = headshot + reel + credits + relationship; office capacity truncates the pile, so good actors are lost structurally, not unfairly.
- **Takes.** 2-5 days per role (est.)
- **Television.** Episodic offices work a 3-7 day gun from breakdown to shoot day; the sort is shallower and the CD's own remembered list does most of the work.

### Agent Pitch & Pressure

The agent works the casting office by phone — pushing named clients, trading on past favours, getting people in who were never on the list.

- **Who.** Talent agent or personal manager
- **Decides.** Whether your representation spends capital on this role or saves it; which of your own agency's clients gets pushed ahead of you for the same part.
- **Engine.** Agent {per-CD relationship score, capital pool, roster with priority order}. A push spends capital, raises P(seen) for that client and lowers it for roster-mates this cycle; capital regenerates when a pushed client books and lands.
- **Takes.** Minutes per call, running the whole casting window
- **Television.** Weekly episodic casting makes these relationships routine and cumulative — the same five offices, every week, for years, so relationship scores dominate TV booking more than film.

### Sides Selection & Release

Two to six pages are chosen as audition sides and posted (historically through Showfax); on secret projects, dummy sides from another script or specially written test scenes are used instead.

- **Who.** Casting director, with the writer's approval
- **Decides.** What the actor gets to show — the crying scene, the funny one, the quiet one — and therefore which of their abilities the read actually measures.
- **Engine.** Scenes carry difficulty tags {emotional load, comic timing, exposition, physical, dialect}. Side choice sets the attribute weighting of every read done from it. Dummy sides decouple read from role and inject a permanent error term into casting decisions.
- **Takes.** Hours
- **Television.** TV sides often come from an episode not yet written, or a prior episode; long-running shows keep a stock of audition scenes that no longer resemble the show.

### Self-Tape Request & Shoot

The office sends sides, instructions and a due time; the actor shoots, picks takes, slates and uploads through the office's platform (Eco Cast or a link).

- **Who.** Casting associate to agent; the actor records at home or in a tape studio, with a reader
- **Decides.** How many takes, which one to send, whether to pay for a studio and a professional reader, whether to add an unrequested second choice, whether to ask for an extension.
- **Engine.** Take loop: each take samples the read vector with variance from nerves, fatigue and reader quality; self-selection of the best take is skill-gated (you cannot pick better than you can judge). Reads self_tape_eyeline, playing_to_the_audition_camera, audio/light quality; late submission = auto-pass at most offices.
- **Takes.** 24-72h turnaround typical; the 2023 SAG-AFTRA terms added page caps, turnaround minimums and no-nudity rules (details est.)
- **Television.** Episodic tapes are frequently due same-day or next-morning, which systematically favours actors with a permanent setup and a reader on call; pilots allow two or three days.

### Self-Tape Review & Selects Grid

The office watches at speed — often the first twenty to thirty seconds — and builds a selects grid per role for producers to review.

- **Who.** Casting director and associate
- **Decides.** As casting: how many seconds each tape gets, and whether look or read wins a tie.
- **Engine.** Attention budget per tape (seconds) x opening-beat quality gates whether the rest is watched at all. Selects = top-k by role fit. Tape-picked actors carry a higher variance term into later live rounds, because no redirect data exists on them.
- **Takes.** 1-3 days per role (est.)
- **Television.** Series offices keep a rolling bring-back list across the season: a tape that just missed in October is next February's guest booking, without a new audition.

### Preread In The Office

The actor comes in, slates to camera, reads with the reader, usually takes one adjustment, and leaves; the tape goes into the grid.

- **Who.** Casting director or associate, with a reader and a camera operator
- **Decides.** The whole live turn loop — what you play first, what you do with the note, whether to ask for another go, when to stop talking.
- **Engine.** Room state {temperature, CD attention, patience clock, your composure}. Each player verb resolves against attributes with variance and updates room state. Output is a read vector (truth, specificity, control, size, listening, look-in-frame, repeatability, direction-taking), never one number.
- **Takes.** 5-12 minutes per actor (est.); sessions of 15-40 actors a day
- **Television.** Faster rooms with more people in them earlier, and for co-star parts the preread is often the only round — the job is decided in eight minutes with no callback.

### The Audition, Played Turn By Turn

Enter, slate, small talk, first read, note, second read, optional third, thank you, leave. Six to fourteen turns, each a choice that spends time, room goodwill or nerve.

- **Who.** Actor (the player), casting director, reader; sometimes director and producers
- **Decides.** Per beat: play as written, commit a specific choice, hold the pause, use the reader or the lens, ask a question, take the note full/half/over, improvise, break and reset, ask to go again, refuse a request, leave early.
- **Engine.** Room holds a hidden want-vector for the role plus temperature and a patience clock. Verb -> deltas on read vector, patience, temperature; notes leak partial information about the want. Take 2 runs a repeatability check against take 1 (repeating_a_callback); reads_nervous and ice_in_the_room modulate variance.
- **Takes.** 3-15 minutes of game time per audition; 6-14 turns
- **Television.** TV rooms are faster, fuller and more comparative — you are read back to back against the others in the hallway — and on a returning series the CD already has a file on you, so the room starts warm or cold before you speak.

### The Redirect

The room gives one note — faster, smaller, play the opposite, drop the tears — and the actor does it again. It is the only part of the audition that measures whether you can be directed.

- **Who.** Casting director; later the director or showrunner
- **Decides.** How far to move. Half-taking reads as not listening; over-taking loses the thing they liked enough to give you a second go.
- **Engine.** Note = target delta on one named axis. Player chooses magnitude {none, half, full, over}. Score = −|applied − wanted| weighted by direction-taking; over-correction subtracts from the axis that got you the note. Directable actors gain a persistent flag the office remembers.
- **Takes.** 60-180 seconds
- **Television.** On episodic the note is often literally schedule pressure — 'faster, we're behind' — and an actor who cannot compress a scene without flattening it fails TV rooms while passing film rooms.

### The Pass, And The Memory Of It

Almost every read ends in no. The office logs why in a private note; the actor usually learns nothing, and the agent gets a sentence if the relationship is good.

- **Who.** Casting director (records it); agent (hears it, sometimes)
- **Decides.** As casting: no for this, yes for the list. As the actor: what you do with it, and whether you send the follow-up note.
- **Engine.** Pass record on both sides {reason code, role, date, read vector}. Roster memory raises P(called in) for future roles even from a pass — the main career ratchet. Actor morale delta modulated by letting_go_of_an_audition and rejection_hangover; post_audition_follow_up nudges CD relationship.
- **Takes.** Instant; the effects run for months to years
- **Television.** In TV the memory pays out fast: a pass on a pilot in March becomes a guest star booking on the same lot in September, from the same office, with no new audition.

### Callback

A short list comes back with more sides and reads for the person who will actually direct them; this is the first time the read is being directed rather than assessed.

- **Who.** Casting director plus the director (film) or showrunner (TV); producers sometimes attend
- **Decides.** Whether to reproduce exactly what got you in or bring something new; what to wear the second time; whether to work with a coach first.
- **Engine.** Callback read = base read x repeatability (does the vector land inside tolerance of the prior one) + director-fit term (director taste vector · read vector). Changing the read raises ceiling and variance together. Coaching spends money and days for a variance reduction.
- **Takes.** 10-20 minutes each; one to two rounds over 1-3 weeks
- **Television.** TV compresses callbacks into days because the test date is fixed, and the taste that matters is the showrunner's, not the director's — episode directors have effectively no casting vote.

### Chemistry Read

Shortlisted actors are paired and read scenes together, often rotating combinations, sometimes against an already-cast lead.

- **Who.** Casting director, director, showrunner; two or more actors in the room together
- **Decides.** As producer: which pairing to push and which strong solo actor to sacrifice for it. As actor: whether you play with your partner or over them.
- **Engine.** Pair chemistry = f(complementarity of read vectors, optics — height, age gap, colouring, off-screen rapport roll, generosity attribute). Explicitly not the sum of solo scores. N x M matrix; the cast solve reads the matrix, so the best individual can lose to a worse actor who pairs.
- **Takes.** Half a day to two days
- **Television.** Series chemistry is tested against the already-cast lead and weighted far higher, because it has to hold for a hundred episodes; film will accept friction that a series cannot survive.

### Producers' Session

A short list reads for the producing team, who are watching for cost, control, and whether this person can carry the days the schedule gives them.

- **Who.** Producers (the player's mother), director, casting director; studio exec sometimes in the room
- **Decides.** As producer: which two names go up to the studio. As actor: whether to push or play it safe with six strangers watching and no note coming.
- **Engine.** Each producer has a want-vector and a weight (money / creative / risk tolerance). Verdict = weighted vote, with disagreement recorded as a state. A split room sends two names up, which burns a week and can lose both actors to other holds.
- **Takes.** 1 day
- **Television.** In TV this is the showrunner's session and the showrunner's vote is usually decisive long before the studio sees the tape; in film a producer can overrule the director on money grounds and often does.

### Test Option Agreement

Before a studio or network test, the actor's entire series or picture deal is negotiated and signed — episodic fee, six years of options, guarantee, billing, exclusivity, relocation — while they still have no job.

- **Who.** Business affairs executive against the actor's agent and attorney, before anyone is cast
- **Decides.** Whether to sign the deal you are handed in order to be allowed into the room, negotiate against a clock at midnight, or refuse and be replaced in the test set.
- **Engine.** Full deal object closed pre-test, MFN-linked across everyone testing for the role. Refusal removes the actor from the test. Player-as-actor gets a take/negotiate/walk decision with a real replacement probability and a real precedent write-through to quoteHistory.
- **Takes.** 24-72 hours, frequently the night before the test
- **Television.** A television institution. Film screen tests rarely require a signed deal first — the offer follows the test — which is why TV actors routinely sign six-year deals for jobs they do not get.

### Studio Test

Two to four actors per role read the same scenes back to back on the same morning in a conference room, for the studio that pays for the show.

- **Who.** Studio casting executive, studio creative executives, producers, showrunner, casting director
- **Decides.** The order you go in (agents fight over it), what you wear, whether to play the room or the scene, and whether to keep the callback read or adjust for a colder audience.
- **Engine.** Comparative scoring — you are scored against the others present, not on an absolute scale — with primacy/recency weighting (est.) and reader fatigue across the block. The studio taste vector differs from the director's; the delta becomes a written note carried into the network round.
- **Takes.** Half a day
- **Television.** The studio-then-network same-day gauntlet is television's. Film's nearest equivalent is a director's on-camera screen test, which is about the face and the pairing, not about approval politics.

### Network / Platform Test

Survivors of the studio test read for the network the same day or the next; the network picks, sometimes in the room, sometimes after a week of silence, sometimes never.

- **Who.** Network casting executive, network president or platform head, showrunner, studio
- **Decides.** As the studio: which two you send and in what order. As the actor: nothing at all, which is the point of the process.
- **Engine.** Network taste vector weighted to franchise and audience fit, constrained by the ensemble being assembled (they will not take two similar leads). Outcomes: cast, held, released, test again with a different partner, role re-opened. Held actors stay off the market unpaid.
- **Takes.** 1-3 hours in the room; decision same day to a week
- **Television.** Purely television. Streaming increasingly replaces the room with an executive tape review and no live read at all, which structurally removes the advantage of actors who are best in person.

### Screen Test On Camera

A real shoot day in miniature: lit, costumed, sometimes on the actual set, to see how a face reads on the chosen format and against the other actor.

- **Who.** Director, DP, hair and makeup, costume designer, casting director, on a stage
- **Decides.** Whether to spend the day, the stage and the crew; for the actor, how far to commit to a version of the character before the job exists.
- **Engine.** Consumes stage, crew and budget from the real schedule. Adds photogenic terms — lens, format, lighting and makeup interact with photogenic_quality and with the other actor's — so someone who won the room can lose on camera, which is the point of testing.
- **Takes.** 2-8 hours; one stage day with a small crew
- **Television.** Rare in TV outside prestige and period series; standard in film for leads and for any role where the look is load-bearing. TV tests faces in the wardrobe fitting instead, with no camera.

### Mix And Match / Ensemble Solve

Winners are not picked one role at a time — the whole cast board is laid out and the room solves for a group that reads as one family, one precinct, one crew.

- **Who.** Director, showrunner, producers, studio and network executives
- **Decides.** Which strong actor you give up to keep the ensemble coherent, and which role you re-open rather than compromise.
- **Engine.** Constraint solve over slots: optics (age gaps, height, colouring, voice contrast), tier balance, total cost within the cast budget, availability overlap, approval constraints. Changing one slot re-runs the solve and can eject an already-favoured actor — visibly, so the player sees why.
- **Takes.** Hours to days, repeated after each test round
- **Television.** TV solves for a title sequence and a decade; film solves for one story. TV will reject a pairing film would happily take, because the pairing has to survive a hundred episodes and a marketing campaign.

### Avail Check, Pin And First Refusal

The office asks whether the actor is free on given dates. A pin or first refusal asks them to hold the dates and to call before accepting anything else.

- **Who.** Casting associate to the agent; the agent decides what to accept
- **Decides.** As the actor's team: whether to accept a pin from a project you do not want and block the one you do; whether to release when a better offer comes.
- **Engine.** Actor calendar of holds {type: avail|pin|first refusal|hold|booked, dates, project, priority}. Conflicting requests force release-or-hold. Releasing burns CD and agent relationship; holding burns real opportunities that the world continues to generate while you wait.
- **Takes.** Minutes to a day to answer; holds run days to weeks
- **Television.** Pilot season stacks holds: actors sit on multiple pilots in first and second position, and a first-position pilot going forward collapses every other hold at once, across several actors, on the same day.

### The Offer

A verbal offer states role, fee, dates, billing and conditions, usually with an expiry; it is accepted, countered or passed on within a day or two.

- **Who.** Producer or business affairs to the agent, after studio/network approval
- **Decides.** The number, what conditions attach, how long the offer stands, and whether it is pay or play.
- **Engine.** Offer object {fee, guarantee, dates, billing, conditions, expiry, pay-or-play flag}. Agent evaluates against quote, tier, competing offers and current heat; bounded counter loop with a walk-away threshold on both sides and a visible clock.
- **Takes.** 24-72 hours; days to weeks for stars
- **Television.** Guest cast offers are frequently 'top of show' — a fixed capped rate — presented on a two-hour clock, take it or we call the next name, because the episode shoots on Monday.

### Deal Negotiation & Points

Beyond the fee: guarantee, overages, travel, per diem, housing, trailer, assistant, publicity days, exclusivity, sequel options, approvals, backend.

- **Who.** Business affairs against the agent, with the actor's attorney and manager in the mail chain
- **Decides.** What you trade. Money for billing, billing for days, days for approval rights, backend for a smaller cheque that protects your quote.
- **Engine.** Multi-issue bargaining with per-side weights and reservation values. Every point carries a real production cost (budget or schedule line) and a real actor value (money, career, comfort). Settlement writes precedent to quoteHistory and creates MFN links to other cast.
- **Takes.** Days to weeks; sometimes still open when shooting starts
- **Television.** Series deals negotiate the whole future at once — six seasons of options, per-season bumps, episode guarantees, relocation, hiatus rights for features — before anyone has seen a single frame cut together.

### Billing & Credit

Where the name goes: above or below title, card position, main title or end crawl, size in paid advertising, 'and' or 'with', favoured nations across an ensemble.

- **Who.** Agent and business affairs; studio marketing has a veto on paid ads
- **Decides.** Whether to take the last 'and' card instead of more money; whether your name is contractually inside the billing block, the trailer and the poster.
- **Engine.** Credit slots are finite objects with tier-dependent value curves. Slot assignment is a constraint solve that must satisfy every executed contract; a later signing can make an earlier contract unsatisfiable, forcing renegotiation or a cash fix — a real, frequent, expensive failure.
- **Takes.** Days to negotiate; disputes run past wrap and into delivery
- **Television.** TV bills a main title that repeats weekly, with 'also starring' and 'special guest star' conventions, and credit order that shifts when an actor's episode count or regular status changes mid-season.

### Pay Or Play

The production guarantees the fee whether or not the picture is made or the actor is ever used — the thing that converts a hold into a commitment.

- **Who.** Business affairs, producer, financier, completion guarantor
- **Decides.** When to go pay or play. Too early and you pay for a film that dies; too late and the actor takes the other job on Thursday.
- **Engine.** Flag with a date. Once set, the fee is a budget liability regardless of production state; abandonment, recast, delay or a moved start all pay out. Cash-flow model, bond company and the greenlight decision all read this flag.
- **Takes.** Decided in hours, usually under duress
- **Television.** TV's version is the episodic guarantee — paid for 7 of 13 whether or not the character appears — plus exclusivity that keeps the actor idle and paid nothing between seasons unless a holding provision says otherwise.

### Station 12 / Union Clearance

Before a performer works, production clears them: are they a member in good standing, are dues current, is this their first union job.

- **Who.** Production payroll or UPM with SAG-AFTRA's membership department
- **Decides.** Nothing discretionary — but it can stop a booked actor the day before they shoot.
- **Engine.** Performer union state {member | eligible | non-member, dues current?}. Clearance runs at booking; failure branches to dues payment, a Taft-Hartley filing, or a recast inside the shoot window with all the cost that implies.
- **Takes.** Same day to 48 hours
- **Television.** Identical mechanically, but episodic volume means it runs dozens of times a season, usually the day before shooting, so failures land at the worst possible moment.

### Taft-Hartley & New Union Members

A non-member can be hired if the producer files a Taft-Hartley report justifying the choice; after thirty days of covered work the performer must join.

- **Who.** UPM and casting director; SAG-AFTRA reviews
- **Decides.** Whether to fight for an unknown who is not in the union and absorb the paperwork, the delay and the possible fine.
- **Engine.** Non-member hire triggers a report plus fine risk (amount est.). Performer gains eligibility, then a must-join clock and an initiation fee they must fund. This rule is what gives street casting and discovery a real cost instead of being free upside.
- **Takes.** Filed within days of the work
- **Television.** Series use it constantly for local day players in the shooting city, and a long run converts a stream of locals into union members, which changes the local labour market the show is standing in.

### Background Casting

Counts per shooting day are ordered off the schedule and filled by look, wardrobe and special ability, with union performers booked first up to the covered count.

- **Who.** Background/extras casting director (Central Casting and equivalents), background coordinator, 2nd AD
- **Decides.** How many bodies per scene, the union/non-union split, what they wear, when they are called, and whether you can afford the crowd the scene was written for.
- **Engine.** Per-day order {count, categories, union split, wardrobe, call time}. Covered-count rules (theatrical roughly 57/day, TV roughly 26/day in covered zones, est.) set the cost curve. Count changes re-price the day and test holding-area, wardrobe and catering capacity.
- **Takes.** Ordered 2-7 days ahead; booked the night before
- **Television.** Series run a standing atmosphere pattern — the precinct, the bar, the newsroom — with a repeat core booked for continuity, so background becomes a recurring payroll and a continuity asset rather than a daily order.

### Vouchers, Upgrades And Eligibility

Vouchers record each background day. A background actor handed a line or identifiable business must be upgraded to day performer on the spot, with the paperwork to match.

- **Who.** 2nd AD, background coordinator, production accountant
- **Decides.** As the AD: whether to give a line to the extra in frame and pay for it. As the background actor: whether to take it and what it does to your week.
- **Engine.** Upgrade event: cost delta = day performer rate − background rate + fringes, plus Taft-Hartley risk if non-union. Advances the performer's union eligibility counter (three union vouchers, est.) — a visible, playable career ladder for an NPC or the player.
- **Takes.** Instant on set; paperwork same day
- **Television.** Long-running series create careers this way: the background regular who gets a line in episode 9 and is a recurring character by season three, with a quote that started at background scale.

### Stand-Ins & Second Team

Stand-ins match a principal's height, skin tone and hair so the crew can light and rehearse camera while the actor is in the chair.

- **Who.** 2nd AD books them through extras casting; the DP and gaffer live with the result
- **Decides.** Matching tolerance, how many, whether the stand-in also photo-doubles, and whether to keep the same people for the whole show.
- **Engine.** Match score on height, skin tone, hair, build against the principal. Lighting minutes saved per setup = f(match quality, stand-in experience). A bad match or no stand-in costs real shoot minutes — a direct, falsifiable line from a casting choice to the day's page count.
- **Takes.** Booked per shoot day; run-of-show for leads
- **Television.** Series stand-ins stay for years, learn the blocking and the show's lighting, and measurably shorten episodic days; film re-hires per production and eats the learning curve every time.

### Doubles: Photo, Body, Driving, Stunt

Doubles cover what the principal cannot or will not do: the far shot, the nude scene, the driving, the fall, the hands on the piano.

- **Who.** Casting director, stunt coordinator (stunt doubles), 2nd AD, costume designer
- **Decides.** Which shots need a double, whose body, what the double is paid, and whether the double gets a credit.
- **Engine.** Per-shot double requirement flag plus double record {match, skill set, stunt grade, rate, credit}. Nudity doubles require a written rider and consent scope; stunt doubles need coordinator approval and feed the injury/insurance model.
- **Takes.** Booked 1-3 weeks ahead; stunt doubles earlier
- **Television.** Series keep doubles on the show for the run; film assembles them per sequence, which means every new film re-tests match quality from scratch.

### Stunt Performer Casting

Stunt performers, drivers and specialists are cast for ability and for match, at scale plus per-gag adjustments negotiated gag by gag.

- **Who.** Stunt coordinator with the director and 1st AD; performers negotiate adjustments individually
- **Decides.** Gag design against the available performer's grade; how many adjustments to pay; whether to shoot it practically at all.
- **Engine.** Gag record {risk grade, required skills, rehearsal days, adjustment cost}. Performer skill vs grade sets injury probability, take count and insurance exposure; an injury cascades into schedule, the actor's own state, and the bond company's attention.
- **Takes.** Cast 2-6 weeks ahead of the sequence; rehearsal days scheduled
- **Television.** Action series carry a core stunt team episode to episode and design gags to what the team can already do; film builds bespoke teams and can afford weeks of rehearsal for one sequence.

### Minors: Casting, Permits And Hours

Child actors need work permits, a Coogan trust (15% of gross in California) and a studio teacher; legal work, school and rest hours cap their day, and infants are cast as twins to double available screen time.

- **Who.** Casting director, studio teacher/welfare worker, parents or guardians, production
- **Decides.** Whether to write the character older to buy hours; whether to cast twins; whether the parent on set is a problem you can live with.
- **Engine.** Minor record {age band -> legal work/school/rest hours per day}. The schedule solver must satisfy it or the day is illegal. Twin casting doubles hours at the cost of two deals and an optics match; parent behaviour is a real on-set friction term.
- **Takes.** Permits take days; the hour constraints last the whole production
- **Television.** A child regular constrains every episode for years and ages out of their own hour band mid-run, so a series' schedule cost from one casting decision changes annually without anyone re-deciding anything.

### Immigration & Work Authorisation

Foreign performers need O-1 or P-type visas supported by a union consultation; the lead time can decide whether a casting choice is possible at all.

- **Who.** Production's immigration counsel, producer, the union (consultation letter)
- **Decides.** Chase the actor you want, or take the one who can legally start on the fourteenth.
- **Engine.** Visa track {type, lead time in weeks, approval probability, premium processing cost}. Compares approval date against start date; failure branches to recast, delay or schedule re-solve. Union consultation is a gate node the production does not control.
- **Takes.** 2-12 weeks typical; premium processing compresses it (est.)
- **Television.** Series must re-clear returning performers season after season and across shooting countries, so a visa problem can remove a regular from a season that is already written.

### Contracts & Start Paperwork

The deal memo becomes a SAG-AFTRA standard employment contract plus riders; the performer signs start paperwork, tax and loan-out documents, nudity riders and NDAs.

- **Who.** Business affairs and legal; payroll company; production coordinator on the day
- **Decides.** Loan-out or individual; which riders you sign and which you strike; which approvals survive from the negotiation into the executed contract.
- **Engine.** Contract object whose clauses are read by other systems: credit slot, nudity limits, exclusivity window, press days, ADR days, approval rights, replica scope. A clause no system reads does not exist and must not be written.
- **Takes.** Days to weeks; day players often sign on set
- **Television.** TV executes one long-form series agreement then issues per-episode paperwork against it, so the terms fought over during the test deal govern work performed years later under a different showrunner.

### Nudity, Intimacy And Consent Terms

Nudity and simulated sex require specific written consent describing what is seen and done; an intimacy coordinator is engaged; nudity is never permitted in an audition or self-tape.

- **Who.** Actor and agent, producer, intimacy coordinator, director
- **Decides.** Exactly what is agreed, scene by scene, shot by shot — and what a refusal on the day costs the schedule and the relationship.
- **Engine.** Per-scene consent record {body areas, acts, camera positions, closed set, double allowed, still photography}. Deviation on the day triggers refuse/renegotiate/walk with real schedule cost. An audition that requests nudity is a violation event with union consequences.
- **Takes.** Negotiated pre-contract; re-confirmed before the day and on the day
- **Television.** Series re-open the conversation every episode because scripts arrive after the deal is signed; a consent negotiated in the pilot does not cover what episode 208 asks for.

### Digital Replica & AI Consent

Scanning a performer or a background crowd for a digital replica requires specific consent and separate compensation under the post-2023 terms.

- **Who.** Business affairs, the actor's attorney, VFX producer, the union
- **Decides.** Whether to sell the scan, for which uses, for how long, and whether it can be used in productions you have not read.
- **Engine.** Scan asset {scope, permitted uses, term, fee}. VFX crowd generation lowers background day cost but consumes consented scans; a refusal removes an option from the VFX system. Scope is re-read at every later use, and expiry is a live event.
- **Takes.** Scan session 1-3 hours; terms negotiated with the deal
- **Television.** Series accumulate scans across seasons, so consent terms expiring mid-run, or a performer leaving on bad terms, becomes a production problem in the middle of a shoot.

### Holds, Start Dates And Conflicting Work

Once cast, the performer is held across a date range; overlapping projects, second positions and drop-and-pick-up rules determine whether a second job is possible at all.

- **Who.** Agent, UPM, production coordinator
- **Decides.** Which job you take when two overlap; whether to ask a production to move your dates and what that request costs you.
- **Engine.** Calendar solver over contract guarantees, DOOD and holds. Drop/pick-up requires a minimum number of intervening free days (est. 10) before production may stop paying a day performer; violating it forces consecutive employment and a larger bill.
- **Takes.** Runs continuously across prep and shoot
- **Television.** Series exclusivity blocks other series outright and permits features only in hiatus with the studio's permission, so a TV job removes a year of film options rather than a few weeks.

### Day Out Of Days & Cast Scheduling

Every cast member's start, work, hold, travel and finish days are mapped onto the schedule; the shape of that grid, not the daily rate, is what cast actually costs.

- **Who.** 1st AD and UPM, off the stripboard
- **Decides.** Whether a guest actor's scenes are grouped into two days or spread across three weeks, and therefore whether you can afford them at all.
- **Engine.** DOOD matrix (cast x day) with codes S, W, H, F, R, T and composites (SW, WF, SWF). Cost = guarantee rules applied over worked, held and travel days. Reordering scenes re-prices cast immediately — the falsifiable link between schedule and casting money.
- **Takes.** Rebuilt on every schedule version
- **Television.** Episodic shows cross-board two episodes at once, so a guest performer may work days in two episodes under two contracts, and a scheduling change in one episode re-prices the other.

### Fittings, Rehearsal And Table Read

Cast are called for fittings, rehearsal and a table read before the first shot; all of it is paid, scheduled, and often where a casting mistake first becomes undeniable.

- **Who.** Costume designer, director or showrunner, production coordinator
- **Decides.** How much rehearsal to buy; whether to recast after the table read and eat the cost.
- **Engine.** Pre-production cast days are real calendar and budget items. Table read produces an ensemble-fit reading that can trigger the recast branch. Rehearsal hours raise on-set take efficiency and lower per-scene performance variance.
- **Takes.** Fitting 2-4 hours; table read half a day; rehearsal 0-3 weeks
- **Television.** Multi-camera comedy runs a full weekly cycle — table read, rewrite, network run-through, rewrite, shoot night before an audience — so casting is re-tested every single week in front of the people who can fire you.

### Recasting & Replacement

A performer is replaced before or during production for performance, conflict, illness, conduct or an executive note.

- **Who.** Producer and studio decide; the casting director executes; the agent hears last
- **Decides.** Eat the pay-or-play cost and reshoot, or live with the performance for the life of the picture.
- **Engine.** Cost = paid-out guarantee + reshoot days + schedule slip + publicity risk roll. Mid-shoot recast invalidates every scene already shot with the original and generates a continuity and VFX bill that the budget model must actually carry.
- **Takes.** Days pre-shoot; weeks and real money mid-production
- **Television.** TV can write a character out instead of recasting, or recast between seasons and either acknowledge it on screen or refuse to — a choice with an audience-perception effect film rarely gets.

### Episodic Guest Cast Cycle

Every episode's guest and co-star roles are broken down, taped, approved and booked on a weekly clock while the previous episode is still shooting.

- **Who.** Episodic casting director, showrunner, studio and network casting executives
- **Decides.** Which roles you spend a name on and which you fill from the office's reliable list; how long you can wait for an executive to answer.
- **Engine.** Rolling weekly pipeline with per-episode deadlines against a shared actor pool, plus a rule blocking the same face playing a second character within a window. Executive approval latency is the modelled failure mode — it eats the days casting needed.
- **Takes.** 5-10 working days from breakdown to call sheet (est.)
- **Television.** This process barely exists in film; the film equivalent is a single day-player sweep in pre-production, done once, with time to think.

### Series Options & Season Pickups

The studio holds options on a regular's services for several future seasons at pre-agreed escalating fees, exercised by a date each year.

- **Who.** Business affairs, studio, showrunner
- **Decides.** As studio: who to option and when to exercise. As actor: whether to demand a renegotiation after a hit, and what that costs in goodwill.
- **Engine.** Option ladder {season, per-episode fee, guarantee, exercise deadline} run against the calendar. Post-hit leverage can force a renegotiation the contract does not require; refusal branches to holdout, written-out character, or a very public walkout.
- **Takes.** Exercise windows each season, typically 30-60 days after a series order (est.)
- **Television.** Film has no equivalent beyond sequel options, which are usually a right of first negotiation rather than a binding multi-year hold at a fixed price.

### Top Of Show & Guest Rate Politics

Episodic television pays major guest roles a capped 'top of show' rate; getting above it is both a negotiation and a public statement about an actor's tier.

- **Who.** Business affairs, agent, showrunner
- **Decides.** Whether to break the cap for a name, and whether you can live with every returning guest asking for the same.
- **Engine.** Rate cap per program length with an override flag; breaking it writes precedent and can trigger MFN claims from returning guests. Guest cast draws from a per-episode allowance inside a season-long pool that the showrunner manages.
- **Takes.** Hours per booking
- **Television.** Wholly a television mechanism. Film's analogue is the scale-plus-ten cameo, where a star works for minimum because they want the part or owe the director.

### Loop Group, ADR & Voice Casting

Crowd voices, radio, off-screen lines and replaced dialogue are cast separately after picture, sometimes matching an on-camera actor who is unavailable or gone.

- **Who.** ADR supervisor, loop group leader, voice casting director
- **Decides.** Bring the principal back on contracted ADR days, or match their voice with someone else and hope nobody hears it.
- **Engine.** Each contract carries ADR day obligations that post-production must schedule or pay for. Loop group booked by voice type and language; voice-match quality feeds the audio quality score and the audience perception model. Player VO work reads voice_audition_recording.
- **Takes.** Group sessions 4-8 hours; principal ADR 2-8 hours per session
- **Television.** Series loop weekly with a standing group who build the show's world for years; film loops in concentrated blocks with a group hired for the picture.

### Local Hire, Travel And Incentives

Whether a performer is hired as a local of the shooting city or travelled changes both the cost of the role and whether their wages qualify for the jurisdiction's tax incentive.

- **Who.** UPM, production accountant, local casting director
- **Decides.** Cast the better actor and fly them, or cast local and bank the credit — per role, thirty times over.
- **Engine.** Incentive rule per jurisdiction {qualifying labour categories, residency test, caps, ATL/BTL treatment}. Casting choice changes incentive yield, which changes the budget; travel adds per diem, housing and travel days as real line items in the DOOD.
- **Takes.** Decided per role during scheduling
- **Television.** Series relocate entire casts for years, so residency, housing, family and school become permanent production obligations rather than a six-week expense.

### Cast Announcement, Trades And NDAs

Signed cast is announced to the trades in a controlled break, or it leaks first out of the breakdown, the session or an agency assistant.

- **Who.** Unit publicist, studio marketing, agent, producer
- **Decides.** When to announce, which outlet gets the exclusive, and what the cast may say publicly while the show is shooting.
- **Engine.** Announcement event feeds the heat system: actor heat delta by tier and role size, project awareness delta, and a quote-inflation effect on that actor's next deal. Leak probability is computed from breakdown channel, session size and NDA state.
- **Takes.** Hours; embargoes of days
- **Television.** TV batches casting news into calendar beats — upfronts, a platform slate event, a Comic-Con panel — so timing is dictated by the network's calendar rather than the production's.

### From Breakdown Line To Call Sheet

The performer is given a cast number, a call time, transport, a dressing room and a work status code, and appears on the call sheet — the moment a line in a breakdown becomes a person standing on a stage at 6:45am.

- **Who.** Production coordinator, 1st AD, 2nd AD, payroll
- **Decides.** Nothing new. It is the audit trail that proves every earlier decision actually held.
- **Engine.** Chain of records: role id -> breakdown -> submission -> audition tapes and read vectors -> offer -> contract -> cast number -> DOOD code -> call sheet row -> timecard -> payroll -> residual record. Each link openable by the player; a break anywhere is a visible bug.
- **Takes.** Call sheets issued the day before, by wrap
- **Television.** The same chain runs again every episode, with regulars persisting and a guest cast list that turns over weekly, so the audit trail is a season-long ledger rather than one document set.

### Discovery, Open Calls And Street Casting

For roles nobody in the files can play, the office runs open calls, scouts schools, theatres, sports clubs and social platforms, and reads people who have never acted.

- **Who.** Casting director, street casting scouts, local casting associates
- **Decides.** How much time and money to spend looking; whether to build a part around a non-professional and accept the risk on the day.
- **Engine.** Search process with cost per week, geographic reach and a hit-rate distribution weighted to role specificity. Non-professionals get a distinct read profile: high truth, low repeatability, low direction-taking, which is exactly what destroys a schedule.
- **Takes.** 2-12 weeks; longer for children and specific ethnic or regional authenticity
- **Television.** TV rarely has the weeks; when it does discovery it is for a pilot lead, and the discovered actor is immediately locked into a six-year option ladder at a beginner's price.

### General Meetings & Relationship Building

A meeting with no role attached — the office gets to know an actor, or a director meets someone for the file. No sides, no camera, no decision.

- **Who.** Casting director, actor, agent; sometimes a director taking generals
- **Decides.** Whether to take the meeting, what to talk about, whether to pitch yourself for something not offered.
- **Engine.** Relationship graph edges {CD or director -> actor, strength, last contact, sentiment}. A general raises P(called in) for future roles and decays over months without contact. Reads working_a_casting_director and casting_director_relations; a bad general can lower the edge.
- **Takes.** 20-45 minutes
- **Television.** TV generals are with showrunners and studio executives who cast across a slate, so one good meeting can put an actor on lists for shows that do not exist yet.

### Essential Element Insurance & Cast Medicals

Leads are examined and declared insurable as essential elements; an uninsurable actor cannot be cast without an exclusion that the financier has to accept.

- **Who.** Production insurance broker, completion guarantor, producer; the performer's doctor
- **Decides.** Whether to cast the actor with the history, pay the loading, buy an exclusion, or walk away from them.
- **Engine.** Performer health/conduct record -> insurability grade -> premium loading or exclusion. An exclusion shifts the risk to the producer; illness or a relapse during the shoot then hits the schedule and the bond, not the insurer.
- **Takes.** Medical days; underwriting 1-3 weeks
- **Television.** Series insure regulars across seasons and re-underwrite at each renewal, so an actor's health or conduct history compounds into a contract problem years after it happened.

### Post-Booking Prep Team

Once cast, the performer assembles or is given a preparation team, some paid by production, some out of their own fee.

- **Who.** The actor, their manager, production; dialect coach, acting coach, trainer, intimacy coordinator, stunt trainer
- **Decides.** Who prepares you, for how many weeks, and who pays — a real negotiation point that continues after the deal closes.
- **Engine.** Prep engagement {type, weeks, cost, payer} with modelled effects: dialect coaching raises accent hold under pressure, training changes physical capability and stunt grade, coaching lowers on-set take variance. Time spent competes with other work and with rest.
- **Takes.** 2-12 weeks pre-shoot; ongoing during production
- **Television.** TV prep is thin and continuous — the dialect coach is on set for years rather than in a room for six weeks — so a TV accent is maintained rather than built.

### Song, Dance And Skill Calls

Musical roles hold singing calls (sixteen bars, then a full number) and dance calls taught on the day; skill roles run practical demonstrations — riding, driving, weapons, instruments.

- **Who.** Music supervisor or composer, choreographer, casting director; specialist coaches
- **Decides.** Whether to cast the singer who can act or the actor who can sing; whether to fake the skill with doubles, cutting and post.
- **Engine.** Skill attributes checked against a role requirement threshold, with a fake-it path costing double days, coverage restrictions and post work. Dance calls run a learn-and-perform loop that reads retention speed, not just ability.
- **Takes.** Dance calls a half day per group; skill demos 30-60 minutes
- **Television.** Musical episodes and competition formats put skill calls inside an episodic clock, so shows cast for skills they will need eight weeks later without knowing the scene.

### Performance Capture Casting

Roles played in a capture volume are cast on body, voice and physical invention, sometimes split between a body performer and a separate voice.

- **Who.** Director, VFX supervisor, performance capture supervisor, casting director
- **Decides.** One performer or two; whether the face is captured with the body; the credit and residual treatment for a performance nobody recognises.
- **Engine.** Capture role {body performer, voice performer, facial capture flag} with rig-fit and physicality attributes distinct from screen photogenics. Split casting creates two contracts, two credits, two replica consent scopes and a synchronisation cost in post.
- **Takes.** Casting 2-6 weeks; capture sessions across the shoot
- **Television.** Animated and capture-heavy series record voice in weekly sessions with actors who may never meet, so ensemble chemistry has to be assembled in the edit rather than found in the room.

### Option spaces

**Breakdown distribution channel** — 12 options

### What gets got wrong

- No figures in this brief could be verified live — the session's web search budget was exhausted and sagaftra.org returns 403 to automated fetches — so every dollar amount, percentage, count and lead time here is marked (est.) and must be re-verified against current SAG-AFTRA rate sheets and the ratified 2023 MOA before it is hard-coded. Structures (categories, clearances, option ladders, DOOD codes) are solid; the numbers are not.
- An audition must not resolve to a single score. Real rooms carry a vector — truth, specificity, control, size, listening, how the face reads in frame, repeatability, directability — and the reason casting exists is that different rooms weight it differently. A scalar makes every casting decision the same decision.
- The room's want-vector has to be hidden and only partially inferable, or the audition is a skill check with a visible answer. The player learns what the room wants from the note they get, which is a single axis, once.
- Repeatability is the axis everyone forgets and every producer cares about: a magnificent read that cannot be reproduced on take four costs shoot days. Model take-to-take variance per actor, and make the callback an explicit repeatability test.
- Self-tapes remove redirect data. A cast built from tapes should carry a higher variance on set than a cast built from live rooms — that is the real cost of the post-2020 shift, and it is what casting directors actually complain about.
- Test option agreements invert the normal order: the actor signs a six-year deal before anyone offers them a job, and refusing to sign means not being allowed to test. If the game lets an actor test first and negotiate later, it is not modelling television.
- Most favoured nations is a contagion mechanic. One actor's bump propagates to everyone MFN-linked, so a single concession at 1am re-prices the whole cast; without the link, favoured nations is just a word in a contract.
- Quote is a ratchet with memory. Working for scale as a favour writes a record other producers will read; quote protection side letters exist precisely to suppress that record, and should be a negotiable point rather than an assumption.
- Pay or play means the money is owed, not that the person is used or credited. Model the three separately: paid, used, credited. Actors are routinely paid and cut out.
- Availability, a pin, a first refusal, a hold and a booking are five different states with five different exit costs. Collapsing them into 'available / unavailable' deletes pilot season, which is entirely about actors sitting in first and second position on competing projects.
- The cast schedule costs more than the cast rate. A guest actor grouped into two days and the same actor spread across three weeks have the same day rate and wildly different bills; if reordering the stripboard does not re-price cast, the DOOD is decoration.
- Background is not one number. Covered counts, union splits, specialty bumps, upgrades and holding-area capacity all move separately, and an AD handing a line to an extra is an instant, expensive, playable decision.
- Stand-ins convert a casting choice into shoot minutes. If a poor match does not cost lighting time, the stand-in is a label; if it does, the 2nd AD's booking becomes a real lever on the day's page count.
- Taft-Hartley and Station 12 are the two places where a booked actor can fail to legally exist on the morning of the shoot. Both are cheap to model and both make discovery casting carry a real cost instead of being free upside.
- Minors do not shrink the scene, they shrink the day. Legal work/school/rest hours must constrain the schedule solver, and twins must genuinely double available hours, or every production casts an eight-year-old lead with no consequence.
- Nudity and intimacy are consent objects with per-scene scope, not a flag on the actor. The playable moment is the deviation on the day — what is asked for that was not agreed — and its cost in schedule, trust and union exposure.
- Digital replica consent has a scope and a term that later systems must re-read. A VFX crowd generated from scans whose consent expired is a real post-2023 production problem and a far better mechanic than a generic 'AI' toggle.
- Billing is a finite-slot constraint problem that can become unsatisfiable. Signing a new actor after two contracts have promised the same card is one of the most common real-world casting disasters and almost never appears in games.
- Television guarantees are counted, not continuous: 7 of 13 means paid for seven whether or not the character appears, and appearing in an eighth costs extra. Film has no analogue and modelling TV as film-with-episodes loses it.
- A series option belongs to the studio, not the actor. Between seasons the actor is exclusive, usually unpaid, and cannot take another series — the defining asymmetry of a television career and the engine of every renegotiation holdout in the industry's history.
- Top of show is a cap, and breaking it is a precedent event, not a payment. If the engine lets a producer pay any number for a guest star with no downstream claim from returning guests, the cap is a label.
- Local hire versus travel is simultaneously a casting decision, a budget decision and a tax incentive decision. If the incentive rules do not read the hire's residency, the entire geography of production disappears.
- Casting directors are not a creative guild in the DGA/WGA sense — in Los Angeles and New York they are represented by Teamsters locals (399 and 817, est.), and the Casting Society is a professional body that gives the Artios Awards, not a union that sets their fees. Getting this wrong makes the CD's own career arc unmodellable.
- Pay-to-play audition workshops are illegal in California under the Krekorian Talent Scam Prevention Act as amended (2017, est.), so 'pay a casting director for access' must be modelled as a legal risk, not as a straightforward career investment.
- Chemistry cannot be computed from two solo scores. If the pairing matrix is separable, there is no chemistry read — there is only addition with a new name on it.
- The three playable roles see different parts of this pipeline, and that asymmetry is the game: the actor never sees the grid, the pass reason, or the room after they leave; the producer sees costs and approvals but not the read; the writer-and-lot-head sees the role as written and the studio's veto. Giving all three the same information collapses the whole domain.
